#154. Restaurant chains refocus on food quality to bring back diners

Juicier chicken tenders. Revamped sauces. Richer and creamier Frosties.

Across the restaurant industry, chains are turning their focus back to their food in the hopes that better quality will win back diners.

Over the last two years, consumers have dined out less frequently and grown pickier about where they choose to eat, based on data from Black Box Intelligence. In that time, "value" has been the buzzword du jour for restaurant executives, who have explained to analysts and investors that the word encompasses more than menu pricing alone.

Now, industry CEOs are turning to their corporate chefs to try to beat rivals on quality and taste, as they look to expand their definition of value. Discounts and one-off promotions can lift traffic for a time, but the actual product still matters for restaurants' long-term growth, as well as franchisee profitability.

From Wendy's to Chipotle Mexican Grill, chief executives touted new or upcoming improvements to menu items on their quarterly earnings conference calls in recent weeks.

"We think the product's improved and even better," Chipotle CEO Scott Boatwright told analysts on the company's call in late October, referring to the burrito chain's brisket, which returned to menus for a limited time Thursday.

For some chains, better food is a key step in ambitious turnaround plans.

For example, "quality food at real value" is one pillar of Wendy's five-part turnaround strategy that the chain unveiled on Thursday.

"We're also rebuilding our food offerings and have begun testing improvements to quality and craveability of our core items, including thicker, crispier bacon, a richer and creamier Frosty, whole leaf lettuce on sandwiches and improved salads," Wendy's CEO Bob Wright told analysts on the call Thursday.

That same day, the company reported its seventh straight quarter of same-store sales declines and publicly rejected a $1.4 billion takeover bid led by Nelson Peltz's Trian Partners. Wendy's said the proposal did not take into account the potential value that its turnaround plan could unlock.

There is precedent to suggest that better-tasting food can contribute to higher sales.

In 2009, Domino's Pizza changed its recipe, swapping out its sauce, adding garlic to its crust and switching to shredded mozzarella with a hint of provolone. Along with blunt commercials that compared its previous crust to cardboard and old sauce to ketchup, the changes helped to spur U.S. same-store sales growth of 9.9% the following year.

Now, some of the same team that led the Domino's turnaround is copying the playbook at Burger King's U.S. restaurants.

Part of the burger chain's multiyear comeback plan has focused on revamping its core menu items. It started earlier this year with its flagship offering, the Whopper, which received a better bun and a new mayonnaise. And in September, it rolled out an overhaul of its nuggets, switching to 100% white meat, crispier breading and a more flavorful marinade. It also reformulated its sauces.

"We've tried to look at our biggest categories first," Josh Kobza, CEO of Burger King parent Restaurant Brands International, told CNBC. "We've also thought about the ones where the fixes are more straightforward and less operationally involved."

Following the changes, Whopper sales were up by a double-digit percentage in the third quarter, Kobza said on the company's earnings call in late October. And nugget sales climbed more than 35%. Burger King said its U.S. same-store sales jumped 8.4% in the quarter.

Like Domino's, Burger King is highlighting diners' past gripes with its Whoppers and nuggets in its ads, which star Burger King U.S. president and former Domino's executive Tom Curtis.

Better-tasting food isn't just for chains that are in trouble.

Even Chili's, which has seen its same-store sales soar 52% over the past three years, is improving more of its existing menu options. After tackling its baby back ribs, burgers and nachos, it will turn next to salads and steaks, Brinker International CEO Kevin Hochman told CNBC.

"We're going to be doing more dry aging of our beef to make it more tender, versus doing it in a plant," he said. "It's a little bit more expensive, but we think the taste is going to be a whole lot better."

Chili's rival Applebee's launched a juicier chicken tender during its latest quarter, according to John Peyton, CEO of the chain's parent company Dine Brands. In 2027, Applebee's plans to overhaul more of its protein options.

"We think proteins are, number one, our biggest opportunity," Peyton told CNBC. "Number two, they're a big focus of the way people are thinking about eating right now, leaning into proteins and a little bit less with the carbs."

Chicken is also a focus for McDonald's, which is testing hand-breaded wings, strips and a sandwich in a handful of Chicago-area restaurants. It plans to expand the trial to more locations in the U.S. and Ireland in early 2027.

Moreover, McDonald's is trying to improve its coffee, with fresher beans and upgraded equipment, executives said at the company's investor day in September. Both chicken and coffee are fast-growing categories, offering more opportunities to lift sales than McDonald's signature beef burgers.

While the burger giant isn't in full turnaround mode like rivals Wendy's and Burger King, its U.S. business has struggled in recent quarters after its value strategy didn't gain the traction the company had hoped. In the third quarter, its U.S. same-store sales fell 0.2%.

In addition to menu tests, McDonald's is also leaning into training to improve the quality and consistency of its core menu items. Starting in October, the company launched "Make It Golden," a multiyear training program for corporate employees, restaurant workers and franchisees.

Ultimately, improved menu items mean little if the recipe changes aren't well executed.

#153. Why the US midterms sparked an AI stock selloff

Is the artificial intelligence (AI) gold rush coming to an end, or is this simply a reality check?

That's the question which is hanging over investors, and which has prompted steep falls on the world's stock markets.

The sell-off in the US on Thursday was most dramatic on the Nasdaq, home to tech industry titans such as Alphabet, Nvidia, Meta and Apple.

It followed sweeping gains by the Democrats in this week's midterm elections in the US, which have given the party full control of Congress.

A number of leading Democrats campaigned against the unhindered expansion of data centres, which have underpinned the growth of artificial intelligence.

Fears this will lead to more regulation of AI appear to have concerned investors. But worries about the industry's prospects have been growing for some time.

The release of Chat GPT by Open AI in late 2022 is widely seen as having generated massive public and corporate interest in the practical uses of AI. It also sparked a modern-day gold rush.

Investors flocked to put their money into companies producing computer chips and networking infrastructure, building data centres and providing the energy to power them, as well as developing the AI itself. The value of tech companies soared.

The boom continued into this year. Investments in AI are expected to reach $1trillion in 2026 alone according to research by Goldman Sachs. The sector has scooped up so much available cash that other industries have seen their own borrowing costs increase as a result.

But investors want returns, and in recent months concerns have been growing about whether the AI boom can produce the expected riches.

Those worries have emerged at a time when public comments from AI researchers casting doubt over the safety of artificial intelligence - even suggesting it could represent an existential threat to humanity - have been grabbing headlines and fuelling speculation that a clampdown could lie ahead.

US tech stocks edge back up after AI slowdown fears sent value of firms plummeting

How does it affect me if share prices fall?

These firms you've never heard of are feeding fears the AI bubble could burst

While the Trump administration has been all-in on AI, or "super intelligence" as the president likes to call it, many leading Democrats tapped into grassroots concerns about the expansion of data centres and their impact on local communities.

These were largely focused on the facilities' high consumption of water and electricity, which many feared would push up their household bills â as well as the noise, use of land, and general disruption associated with them.

A Gallup poll published in May showed 71% of Americans would oppose the building of a data centre in their community. The figure for a nuclear power station was 53%.

James Talarico, who won the Senate race in once staunchly Republican Texas, was one of those to respond to this scepticism.

"While we want to protect America's leadership in artificial intelligence, we are still grappling with how it will affect everything from how we work to how our children learn," he said.

"We must protect Americans and establish clear, consistent laws for businesses."

With control of both houses of Congress, Democrats are now expected to challenge the president's technology agenda more vigorously, while introducing legislation which could curb the expansion of the industry.

Texas' Democratic governor-elect Gina Hinojosa campaigned against data centres, pledging a moratorium on the construction of new facilities.

Hinojosa's win could present opposition to tech multi-billionaire Elon Musk, who has moved large parts of SpaceX and Tesla's operations to the state, for years a Republican stronghold.

The Tesla boss had spent nearly $450m to back Republican campaigns in the midterms.

As the co-founder of the British-based technology firm Arm Holdings, Tudor Brown, points out, "stock values are based on future earnings not present earnings". If the rewards are not likely to be as great as expected, some investors will choose to sell up.

The falls seen on stock markets over the past 24 hours would appear to have been triggered by the midterm results. But concerns that tech firms have become overvalued were being expressed long before the elections.

The question now is whether this is simply the removal of froth from an over-exuberant market, or a sign that the AI rush is a bubble about to burst, with much wider consequences.

Nandini Roy Choudhury of Future Market Insights thinks investors will assess whether the political shift could result in restrictions on data centre construction, energy consumption and the deployment of AI technology.

She adds there is unlikely to be an immediate widespread recovery in tech stocks as traders are likely to be more selective, favouring firms with stronger balance sheets.

Brown thinks the impact will be limited. Share valuations, he thinks, were inflated by expectations of "almost infinite AI growth", which have been followed by "a bit of a panic".

"There is a natural floor to this", he says. "After that it will reflect I think that actually there's still a lot of money being made in these companies".

#152. He spent $50,000 on Labubus but now the craze is dying

Ensito, with his custom-made Labubu teeth, posing with a new doll in his collection

For years, Labubu dolls seemed unstoppable.

Collectors young and old energetically snapped up "blind boxes" - sealed, mystery packages containing the quirky monsters - often paying $15 (Â£11) or more.

Those unable to get the characters they wanted were often prepared to pay much more on the resale market.

The hype around the creatures convinced a mega-fan from North Carolina, who goes by the name Ensito, to leave his tech job in 2024 to create Labubu content full-time, travelling as far as Italy and Spain to seek out the latest releases.

Ensito has spent more than $50,000 (Â£37,000) to build his collection of more than 400 Labubus, a figure he tells the BBC he's "embarrassed to say".

He even had a row of custom Labubu-like jagged teeth made by a dentist to wear at events.

But in recent months, Ensito's once-viral posts about the furry monsters have struggled. "Those videos used to be able to hit 10,000 really quickly but I'm struggling to do that now."

Ensito's focus has shifted to other trends like Hello Kitty and squishies.

It's a sign that the craze which made parent company Pop Mart worth more than US toy giants Hasbro and Mattel put together may be running out of steam. Analysts believe the Beijing-based brand is struggling to capture excitement beyond a single viral moment.

Pop Mart recently reported that global sales in the three months to the end of September had fallen by as much as 50% on the previous year.

The drop was particularly steep outside mainland China, with US revenue down by at least 70%.

Pop Mart didn't invent blind boxes but its dolls certainly helped to popularise them around the world.

The sales tactic made collecting feel "nostalgic", evoking the feeling of opening gifts as a child, says marketing professional Katelynn Carlson, who discovered Labubus through social media and now owns 36 of them.

One of her first Labubus was a rare doll, making the experience "even more exciting", she adds.

Labubu, which launched in 2019, has been Pop Mart's most successful line of toys. The monsters became fashion statements and were sported by celebrities including Kim Kardashian and Blackpink's Lisa.

They regularly attracted long queues and became so popular that Pop Mart had to temporarily pull the toys from its UK shops after reports of customers fighting over them. One fan paid more than $150,000 for a human-sized Labubu.

A key part of the allure was how difficult some Labubu dolls were to find, with fans repeatedly buying blind boxes to get the version they wanted, says National University of Singapore marketing lecturer Samer El Hajjar.

Katelynn Carlson believes blind boxes were an exciting feature of the Labubu craze

Pop Mart now has 500 shops in more than 30 countries and 2,000 vending machines worldwide. The firm, which listed in Hong Kong in 2020, is worth nearly $24bn and remains one of the world's most valuable toymakers, despite its shares having fallen by more than 50% from last year's peak.

Investors worry its global success was just a viral moment rather than sustainable growth driven by long-term customer loyalty, El Hajjar says.

For Ensito, it is clear the dolls' exclusivity helped fuel the craze.

"Now it doesn't feel that way. I walk into a Pop Mart and there's a whole series of Labubus sitting there," he says.

Ashley Dudarenok of China research firm ChoZan says that sense of exclusivity faded as Pop Mart ramped up production to meet demand.

Pop Mart declined to comment on share price moves and did not respond to questions about falling sales.

As the Labubu mania cools, some of its rivals have become more popular.

Blind box collectables like Sonny Angels - tiny, unclothed cherub-like dolls - and miniature glow-in-the-dark Smiski figurines have been flying off shelves. And you can now find My Little Pony and KPop Demon Hunters in blind boxes too.

Miniso, founded in the southern Chinese city of Guangzhou, sells a range of blind-boxed Disney and Marvel characters in more than 8,000 shops globally.

"Consumers aren't particularly concerned about where the brand comes from," Miniso executive Vincent Huang said earlier this year. "They're more focused on the shopping experience - the designs, value for money, and enjoyment."

Pop Mart faces competition from retailers like Miniso

Experts say one of Pop Mart's biggest challenges is creating enduring characters.

That's an area where Hello Kitty brand Sanrio, which posted record earnings in the year to March, has a clear advantage, analyst Boustani suggests.

Pop Mart has invested heavily in ways to grow its brand, including setting up a theme park in Beijing and announcing a Labubu film - following in the footsteps of franchising giants like Disney or Lego, some experts believe.

But unlike Disney, which sells a story before it sells a toy, Pop Mart is working in reverse, says Dudarenok. "The real test for next year is whether Pop Mart can make people love a character without a celebrity holding it."

Glow-in-the-dark Smiskis are emerging as a competitor to Labubus

After spending huge sums on the monsters, collector Ensito says he is now questioning how much time and money people invest in collections each time a new trend comes along. "There's always going to be a trend every year. There's always gonna be something that's gonna be really popular."

Melbourne-based content creator Lawrence Yu says that after two years of chasing Labubus, he's likely to be "more selective" before adding to his roughly 50 dolls.

His Labubu shopping and unboxing clips once drew up to a million views but the frenzy has faded and Yu rarely makes videos about them now. "I definitely think the trend has slowed down... People go crazy and then it fizzles."

Clara Barbero from Canada believes Labubus are a "dead trend" but is still keeping an eye out for interesting releases.

"Iâm curating my collection more and only buying what I see myself liking for the next few years, and not just buying the newest series thatâs out."

Lawrence Yu says he has spent about $1,400 on Labubu dolls

But the fluffy monsters still have truly devoted fans.

Hedy Khaled from Liverpool says although the hype around the dolls is cooling, she is just as attached to her collection of 70 Pop Mart figurines.

There's a distinction between those who chase trends and "real fans", she argues, so falling sales do not necessarily mean Labubus have lost their appeal.

For Carlson says it is the happiness the dolls bring, not the viral trend, that keeps her collecting. She still gets excited about new Pop Mart releases, like its recent cat-themed Labubu series, which has become one of her favourites.

"I think there's something special about embracing our inner child and enjoying things simply because they make us happy."

'I've spent more than Â£3,000 on Jellycats'

'I have the world's biggest My Little Pony collection'

#151. This beaten-down data center stock could be worth a look, says Mike Khouw

I'm a firm believer in investing in what you know—and what you use. During a recent visit to my parents' New Hampshire property, my brother and I tackled overdue maintenance, and having the right equipment on hand was critical to finish the work in the limited time we had.

He and I rented both a compact track loader (CTL, not pictured) and a bulldozer (pictured above) from United Rentals (NYSE: URI) to push through some of the heavier work.

For those who may not be familiar with the company, United Rentals can quickly deploy heavy equipment to a remote site. Here's why I believe it sets up well for a defined-risk, moderately bullish options trade.

The good news is that United Rentals is currently trading at a below-market forward multiple — both on a Price-to-Earnings (P/E) basis, currently ~17.3x forward estimates of ~$61.10/share, and on an Enterprise Value-to-EBITDA (EV/EBITDA) basis at ~8.8x $9.25 billion in FYE2027 EBITDA. This despite the fact that the company is compounding revenues, earnings, and EBITDA at a noticeably faster clip than the cap-weighted S&P 500. Effectively, one can buy superior, market-beating growth at a discounted market multiple.

The recent third-quarter earnings confirmed this trajectory. URI delivered a beat and raise, pointing to robust underlying demand across its end markets, and the sell-side is scrambling to catch up, revising its forward estimates upward. Improving earnings estimates is one of the most reliable, time-tested short-term investment factors. Stocks with positive estimate revisions tend to meaningfully outperform those burdened with declining estimates in the short term.

The macro backdrop provides a sturdy foundation for this growth. We are in the midst of a broader nonresidential construction recovery, which has driven an environment of improving and resilient rental rates. Although admittedly a potential slowdown in data center construction could become a future headwind, broader infrastructure demand remains robust.

A balanced view requires acknowledging the risks. While URI looks cheap relative to the broader market, it is not exactly cheap relative to its own history. The stock is currently trading roughly 20% to 25% above its five-year average multiple. One could easily argue that the historical multiple was simply too cheap for a business of this quality, scale, and capital efficiency. Still, mean reversion back to historical levels of "cheapness" remains a downside risk.

This brings us to the options market. When you have a stock with solid momentum, fundamentals, and positive estimate revisions, but you're hesitant to pay a premium to its historical valuation, outright unhedged equity accumulation isn't always the optimal route. Instead, selling cash-covered puts offers a highly strategic entry mechanism.

With URI's implied volatility currently hovering in the 42% to 45% range, the options market is pricing in enough movement to offer a decent premium. By selling a cash-covered put, we acknowledge both the good and the bad. It respects the aggressive growth, market share capture, and the recent beat-and-raise quarter, while appropriately hedging against the risk of that elevated historical multiple.

If the stock continues its upward march, the puts expire worthless, and we pocket a high-probability yield that easily beats fixed income. If the stock pulls back—perhaps toward that 5-year average multiple—we are assigned the shares at a lower effective cost basis, which, if it happens, we could lower even further by writing upside covered calls against it. (This approach to entering a stock, then writing covered calls if assigned, is sometimes referred to as "the wheel strategy").

So either we acquire an attractive stock at a price we dictate (the short strike, less the premium collected for selling it) or we simply collect premium for assuming that risk if the stock does not decline. For a company that provides the literal heavy lifting for infrastructure growth, that's a structural advantage worth trading.

#150. 'My mortgage is going up £200 a month': How rising interest rates are hitting people's pockets

The BBC spoke to three people about how they could be affected by rising interest rates

On Thursday, the Bank of England increased interest rates for the first time in over three years as it tries to get a grip on rising inflation linked to the Iran war.

Experts say there could be more hikes over the next 18 months, as the conflict continues to push up food, fuel and energy prices across the UK and worldwide.

Higher interest rates affect borrowing costs, however, which means mortgages, credit cards and personal loans could get more expensive for millions, even as savings rates improve.

For some, it could mean extra financial pressure at a time when they are already struggling with the cost of living. The BBC spoke to three people about how they could be affected.

Peter Gill says his family's costs are rising

Peter Gill, 48, is one of the around 1.3 million Britons on a variable rate mortgage.

These loans track the Bank of England base rate, with a margin on top determined by the lender, so any rise in interest rates can push up repayments.

Peter, who runs a small tiling business in Halifax, says his monthly repayments will go up by roughly Â£45 following Thursday's quarter-percentage point increase and he's worried they could climb higher.

But he still thinks it'd be more expensive to move onto a fixed-rate deal - which is when you pay a set amount of monthly interest for a period - and is prepared to stay put for now.

Watch: What does the raise in interest rates mean?

His higher mortgage costs come as the "cost of everything is going up", he says.

The father of two, who has a combined household income of Â£45,000 with his wife Faye, is having to pay his staff more because of increases in the national minimum wage. Raw material costs have also risen due to the war in Iran.

At home, the family's food, gas and electricity bills have gone up and he says he's working "about six and a half days a week" to shield them from the worst of it.

"From my point of view, inflation goes up which costs you, then interest rates go up and you have even less money."

Dawn Fazackerley-King is struggling but says there's "no more fat to trim"

Most UK mortgage holders are on fixed-rate deals, but that doesn't necessarily protect them when the Bank of England puts up rates.

David Hollingworth, from brokers L&C Mortgages, says many people will be shocked at how much higher fixed-rate deals are than they were five years ago following recent increases.

"Fixed rates have already risen due to market anticipation that interest rates would need to rise to combat higher inflation," he says.

Dawn Fazackerley-King is a Pilates instructor who lives near Ipswich and has previously spoken to the BBC about her struggles with the cost of living.

She was on a five-year fixed deal at 1.4%, which she took out in January 2022. But the mother of two is about to refix for two years and the best deal she can find is a 5.69% loan. That will push her costs up by about Â£200 a month.

"I don't know where that money is going to come from, it's something we are going to have to figure out as a family. It's stressful."

She and her husband, who have a joint income of about Â£70,000, have already gone through their direct debits and there's "no more obvious fat to trim".

There is also no scope for them to take on additional work, as they both work full time.

"We're still living in the same property, our household costs are remaining the same," Dawn says. "It's an additional cost of several hundred pounds but we're not getting anything extra for that."

Higher interest rates are "another cost to deal with", says Ben

When the Bank of England increases its base rate, credit card borrowing costs also tend to increase, although the impact is not always immediate.

It may affect people with outstanding debts, as providers can increase the interest rates they charge with 30 days' notice.

Ben Thomas, 41, from Shropshire, ditched his career as a chartered accountant during the Covid pandemic after a mental health crisis and retrained as a paramedic.

But the drop in income left him reliant on credit cards and personal loans for everyday living expenses and he is now Â£52,000 in debt.

Faisal Islam: How high will interest rates go?

Three ways the interest rate rise could affect you

Ben says he's reached an agreement to pause the interest on most of it, but he is still incurring charges on around Â£2,000 of credit card debt and is worried about his repayments going up.

"With all that's going on, rising interest rates is another cost to deal with," he tells the BBC.

As well as overall debt repayments of around Â£300 a month, he and his wife pay Â£760 on their mortgage. They have also struggled with higher food and energy bills.

Simon Trevethick is head of communications at StepChange, which has been helping Ben manage his debt. He says the debt charity has seen a 20% increase in people asking for support this year and warned of tougher times ahead.

"If higher borrowing costs feed through into household budgets over the coming months, we could see further pressure on people seeking support."

Do you have a mortgage, a credit card, a car finance agreement? How are interest rates affecting you?

#148. Government 'looking seriously' at banning under-16s from Vinted and eBay

Vinted and eBay could be included in the list of platforms set to be banned for children aged under 16, the culture secretary has said.

Lisa Nandy said the government was "looking seriously" at including the retailing websites in the social media ban "to keep children safe".

The UK's social media ban for under-16s is set to come into force in March next year, with children barred from accessing platforms such as Instagram, TikTok and Snapchat.

In response, eBay said it was looking forward to "understanding more and continuing to work constructively with the government". Vinted said it welcomed "open dialogue" with ministers and the regulator on the issue.

Asked on the BBC's Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg programme whether the government was looking to ban under-16s from Vinted and eBay, Nandy said: "We are looking seriously at doing so in order to keep children safe."

It came after the Sunday Times reported, external that the UK's social media ban was being expanded to include the popular second-hand online marketplaces.

The move was due to fears that messaging functions on the platforms allowed strangers to groom children, according to the newspaper. It suggested similar reselling platforms, Depop and Facebook Marketplace, would also be included in the list of banned sites.

Businesses and individuals sell a host of goods on eBay, which has been a major global player in the online reselling market for years. The platform states on its website that it currently requires all of its members to be over 18 years old because listings involve "entering into a contract".

Vinted is aimed at over 18s, but its terms and conditions do allow parents or guardians to create accounts for their children, which must be used under parental supervision.

The site, on which users mostly buy and sell unwanted clothes and other fashion items, has grown rapidly recently to reach 17 million UK users.

A Vinted spokesperson said direct messaging was "part of how the platform works" but that it had a "zero tolerance for harmful or explicit content" and had moderation teams and tools to spot and remove "activity that breaks our rules".

"When we identify inappropriate behaviour, we take action, up to a permanent ban or reporting to the relevant authorities," they said.

An eBay spokesperson said the company was "focused on providing a trusted and safe marketplace experience for our millions of customers".

"While the details of the proposed legislation are not yet clear, we look forward to understanding more and continuing to work constructively with the government," they added.

The government announced it would ban under-16s accessing social media in June this year, with children prevented from accessing 10 of the biggest platforms.

YouTube will also be banned for under-16s, but YouTube Kids - a version of the video-sharing app designed to be safe for children - will not be included in the measures.

A complete list of affected platforms has not yet been released, but the government said it would cover those "whose purpose is to enable social interaction and which allow users to post material".

That means platforms that have livestreaming or features that enable the potential for strangers to contact children will also be restricted, which includes gaming sites.

It does not mean that Roblox, the biggest gaming site in the UK for those aged eight to 12, for example, will be banned for under-16s. However, certain features such as its chat function will be restricted.

But Matthew Sinclair, a senior director at the Computer and Communications Industry Association, which represents tech companies, argued that ministers should prevent the rules having "scope creep".

He said a restriction on messaging for under-16s "would not stop at social media and would reach into the services families depend on to stay in touch", and urged the government to "ensure any new restrictions are proportionate and effective."

The UK's ban comes after a similar one was introduced in Australia last year.

Many children there have found ways to get round it, but the UK has said it intends to take a tougher approach.

In recent weeks, attention has turned to how a social media ban would be enforced.

Last month, the communications regulator Ofcom set out its recommendations for the most effective ways to identify which of the UK's millions of social media users are aged over 16.

Rani Govender, head of online child safety at the NSPCC, said the "real question" going forward was how the government ensured age checks were effective.

"But effective age checks are only part of the solution," she added. "Tech companies must also be required to build child safety into every platform, device and AI tool, so children are protected from harmful and illegal content and only able to access age-appropriate services."

Are you a parent? Do you have any views or questions on this story?

#146. A massive trade in Nvidia just went off – here are the details

The biggest options trade on Nvidia stock Monday seems to think the controversy swirling around the chip leader and its embattled CEO is creating a buying opportunity.In a trade about an hour after the opening bell that involved more than $100 million in options premium, someone exchanged an existing call spread for a more bullish position that captures the next five months of trading in Nvidia.The core position was the purchase of 52,000 195-strike calls expiring Feb. 19 worth about $57 million – contracts the trader opened for a new long position while simultaneously selling the same number of 245-strike calls for the same expiry, lowering the cost of the trade by $10 million. The net position, a bullish call spread, needs Nvidia to trade above around $206 by expiry, or about 15% higher, to be profitable. If Nvidia stock reaches the short, 245-strike call, it would represent a nearly 40% rally and return the stock to its all-time high of $243, that was reached on October 6th.The trade was likely part of a "roll" down from an existing 210/255-strike call spread worth about $42 million that was closed out, according to SpotGamma analysis.

"The person got more aggressive rolling this spread down to lower strikes to get more upside exposure by getting higher delta," SpotGamma founder Brent Kochuba said by phone. "They're trading the call spread here because the vol is expensive going into earnings. It's a significant increase in upside delta exposure."Delta is options parlance for describing how quickly an options price will react to a change in the underlying stock price. The trader's updated position, taken at lower strikes, will make money faster if Nvidia rallies. In other words, it's a higher conviction trade that the chipmaker's stock will soon rebound. The 195-strike calls they bought for $57 million was the single biggest options trade on the tape Monday, according to SpotGamma. Of the top 20 options trades by dollar-amount, 12 were Nvidia, the data show.Flows around the stock generally leaned bullish by midday, with 57% of options volume tied to calls and more than $660 million of call premium likely initiated by call-buyers, SpotGamma data show. The most popular single contract by volume was the 180-strike call expiring Monday, that needs Nvidia to recover 1.2% by the bell to break even.

#145. Eli Lilly’s Foundayo obesity pill eases sleep apnea in late-stage trial, paving way for approval

Eli Lilly on Monday said its obesity pill Foundayo significantly reduced the severity of obstructive sleep apnea, the most common sleep-related breathing disorder, in patients with obesity, in a late-stage trial.

The drugmaker plans to file by the end of the year for U.S. approval of the daily pill for moderate-to-severe obstructive sleep apnea, or OSA, in adults with obesity or who are overweight. The results make the pill — which rolled out in the U.S. in April — one of several GLP-1 treatments to show promise in treating the condition.

OSA refers to interrupted breathing during sleep due to narrowed or blocked airways, which can cause drops in oxygen and increases in blood pressure that are associated with serious complications, such as hypertension, heart failure and stroke. The condition affects nearly 1 billion adults globally, including 80 million people ages 20 and over in the U.S., according to the National Council on Aging. Most have never been diagnosed, Lilly said in a release.

Lilly's blockbuster weight loss injection Zepbound became the first medication to win U.S. approval for OSA in December 2024. Patients have limited treatment options outside of that drug and many use cumbersome machines that provide positive airway pressure, or PAP, to allow for normal breathing.

"Foundayo is already making a difference for people living with obesity, and with plans to submit these results to the FDA by the end of the year, we could have the opportunity to help people living with OSA," Dr. Thomas Seck, senior vice president of product development of Lilly Cardiometabolic Health, said in a release.

The trial tested Foundayo in two independent studies, the first of which included 363 patients who were unable or unwilling to use PAP therapy. The second study included 349 people who were on PAP therapy for at least three months at the time of screening and continued that treatment during the trial.

Patients in both studies who took Foundayo started at a low dose and increased it gradually at four-week intervals to different maximum dosages.

Researchers specifically examined how much the pill reduced the so-called apnea-hypopnea index, or AHI, which records the number of times per hour a person's breathing shows a restricted or completely blocked airway. The index is used to evaluate the severity of obstructive sleep apnea and the effectiveness of treatments for the condition.

People who took Foundayo and were not using PAP therapy had an average of 21.1 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 4.8 per hour for the placebo, according to the results.

Foundayo also met the trial's second goal in that group, leading to an overall AHI reduction of 43.8% compared with a decrease of 6.3% for the placebo. Lilly said 31.5% of the participants in the Foundayo group met the criteria for remission or improvement to mild OSA.

Meanwhile, patients who were on both Foundayo and PAP therapy had an average of 18.3 fewer AHI events per hour at 52 weeks, compared with an average reduction of 5.5 per hour for the placebo.

Foundayo led to an overall AHI reduction of around 27.5% in that group, compared with a decrease of 3% for the placebo. The company said 19.6% of the participants on Foundayo and PAP therapy also met the criteria for remission or mild OSA.

Foundayo also reduced what's known as the sleep apnea-specific hypoxic burden — a measure of how deeply and for how long oxygen levels fall during sleep. That metric fell by 55.9% in people not using PAP therapy and 45.8% in those using it.

Lilly said the pill also helped patients lose roughly 11% of their weight across groups at 52 weeks and helped to lower a marker of inflammation.

The company said the safety data and how well patients tolerated Foundayo were generally consistent with previous trials on the pill.

Around 12% of people on Foundayo who were not using PAP therapy discontinued due to side effects, compared to 3.8% with the placebo. Meanwhile, 6.8% of those on both Lilly's pill and PAP discontinued the drug due to side effects, compared with 0.6% in the placebo group.

The most common side effects were gastrointestinal, including nausea, diarrhea, constipation and vomiting, and were generally mild to moderate in severity. That's consistent with other GLP-1 medications.

#143. Government's EV targets expected to be reduced this week

The government launched a review into its policy to ban new petrol and diesel car sales by 2035

The UK government's electric vehicle (EV) targets are expected to be reduced this week in a move likely to be welcomed by carmakers but opposed by environmental groups.

Under the current rules, 80% of new cars sold in the UK must be electric by 2030 but industry groups have long called for "urgent relief".

New petrol or diesel cars are due to be phased out by 2030 and by 2035 all new cars must be zero emission.

But the speed of the transition to electric has been hotly debated, including within the cabinet.

The government has been consulting on reducing its 80% rule and a significant reduction is now expected.

Downing Street has insisted no decision has been made.

A government source told the BBC it would approach the issue in a "pragmatic and balanced" way.

If ministers do decide on a steeper watering-down of the rules, it will arguably be interpreted by some - alongside the approval of the Jackdaw gas field in the North Sea - as the government prioritising economic considerations over environmental ones.

Prime Minister Andy Burnham has recused himself from this issue due to his wife's links to an electric vehicle company.

The key meetings on the subject will instead be chaired by Cabinet Office minister Louise Haigh.

Asked about reports the targets would be reduced, Burnham dismissed this as "speculation", adding: "No decision has been made at this point."

Sustainability groups say any weakening of the 2030 target will threaten the UK's long-term electrification and climate goals.

It could undermine the UK's claim to be providing leadership on climate just as ministers head to Turkey for COP31, the UN's climate summit.

Energy UK, the trade association for the energy industry, has also opposed the move.

Last month, it said, external reducing targets could damage "the investment certainty needed to deliver the infrastructure, innovation and services required for the transition to zero-emission transport".

Asked if climate change was no longer a big priority, Health Minister Karin Smyth said the government was committed to its emissions targets.

"This country is doing great work in terms of our transition and looking at renewables â that's a key part of growth in different parts of the country," she told BBC Breakfast.

She added that the prime minister would have "a good story to tell" when meeting other leaders at COP31.

A government spokesperson said: "We are committed to the phase out of all new cars relying solely on petrol and diesel engines by 2030, and to 100% of new car and van sales being zero emission by 2035, and our legally binding net zero targets."

The Society of Motor Manufacturers and Traders welcomed the government's review of the EV targets when it was announced in the summer.

Last week, head of the association Mike Hawes said transitioning to electric vehicles had come at a "heavy cost" with billions spent on discounts to drive demand in electric vehicles.

He said the government's targets could not be achieved "without putting at risk any future investment".

UK electric car sales target set to be weakened

Electric vehicle sales targets could be cut after pressure from car makers

EU waters down plans to end new petrol and diesel car sales by 2035

Net zero means balancing the amount of planet-warming "greenhouse" gases produced by human activities with the amount being actively removed from the atmosphere.

The government's policy to phase out petrol and diesel vehicle sales has undergone multiple changes since it was first introduced by former Prime Minister Boris Johnson in 2020.

His successor Rishi Sunak pushed back the timeline to 2035, and the Burnham government set up a review of the EV sales targets in August.

Sunak introduced phased targets for EV sales in the UK, known as the Zero Emission Vehicles (ZEV) mandate.

Under the ZEV mandate, the percentage of new car sales that need to be EVs increases each year. The target was 28% for 2025, 33% for 2026, and so on until it reaches 80% by 2030.

Pressure in the European Union saw the bloc scrap a similar plan to ban new petrol and diesel car sales by 2035, particularly from countries with large auto industries such as Germany.

The Conservative Party has also vowed to abandon the petrol car ban if it wins the next election, with leader Kemi Badenoch calling it a "well-meaning, but ultimately destructive piece of legislation".

Green Party leader Zack Polanski said the government was "building up a shocking record of backsliding and bad decisions on climate issues".

#138. Goodyear Tire CEO Mark Stewart steps down as company continues turnaround plan

Goodyear Tire & Rubber Co. announced Monday that CEO Mark Stewart would be stepping down from his role effective immediately.

Board member Roger Wood is taking over as interim CEO and president, Goodyear said.

The leadership change comes as the tire company is in the midst of a multiyear transformation plan.

Stewart was hired in January 2024 to lead the company, with the goal of revitalizing the legacy American business to make it more efficient and profitable.

The company has faced disappointing results thus far, reporting last week third-quarter results that saw net sales down 2.3% year over year and an adjusted net loss of $31 million. Goodyear failed to reach a 10% operating margin by the end of last year, which was a major goal of the turnaround plan.

Goodyear said it formed a special committee of the board earlier this year to conduct a "strategic review of the company's businesses and operations focused on identifying opportunities to strengthen performance and drive shareholder value."

It said Monday it will continue that work by evaluating strategic and operational initiatives that include further optimizing the company's business mix and footprint, improving efficiency and investing in new products and capabilities that support long-term growth.

"Goodyear has made meaningful progress in becoming a more efficient and focused company, and the board believes this is the right moment to transition leadership to accelerate that progress and capture additional opportunity," Goodyear Chair Joe Hinrichs said in a press release.

The company has faced headwinds from tariffs, higher raw material costs and increased competition from China.

Wood has been a member of the company's board of directors since July 2023 and was previously the co-CEO of Tenneco as well as CEO of Dana Holding Corporation.

The company will start a search for a permanent replacement as part of its strategic review.

Shares of Goodyear were down roughly 4% in early trading Monday.

#136. Doing these things now will help you save money at Christmas

How soon is too soon when it comes to thinking about Christmas and your finances?

The answer, of course, is that it is never too soon. Save early and the cost becomes more manageable.

The squeeze is harder for some than others, with charity Barnardo's warning that some parents will skip meals so their kids get a Christmas dinner with the trimmings.

But, debt advice charity StepChange is offering tips to everyone, to avoid Christmas spending leading to a financially painful new year.

The best way to avoid a difficult January, it says, is to make a realistic plan before the spending begins.

Small decisions now can make a big difference later. Its tips include:

Budget for Christmas in full. So, that's not just presents, a budget should include the cost of food and drink, travel, parties, presents, postage, decorations and other festive extras

Spread the cost. Don't just rely on your December paypacket. Also, make a shopping list before Black Friday on 27 November - it is only a discount if you were going to buy it anyway

Book your train journey now. Advance tickets, if available, are cheaper

Set spending limits. Put a cap on the bill for Christmas socialising, and have a quiet conversation with family and friends about how much to spend on presents

Stocktake before the big food shop. Check your cupboards, fridge and freezer before you go shopping and plan meals around food you already have. Make a list of what you need and compare prices.

Think twice. Can you afford what you are going to buy? Can you afford the repayments if it's bought on credit? And, do you really need it?

"Christmas is a time when we want to enjoy ourselves and make things special for the people we care most about," said Vikki Brownridge, chief executive of StepChange.

"But it can also put a lot of pressure on household budgets, and it is key to take steps to get ahead of any January financial blues."

If you are concerned about Christmas leaving you in debt, then it is best to seek advice well before things become unmanageable.

The independent MoneyHelper website has a guide to finding free debt advice near you, external.

There are also tools to work out if you are receiving all the benefits and grants you are entitled to, and tips on how to draw up a budget.

And it is really important to watch out for scams at this time of year. Fraudsters will take any opportunity to catch you out, especially when you are under pressure financially.

How have you been affected by the issues raised in this story? Get in touch.

#134. Tokenization platform Securitize gains 10% postmarket on Q3 results, move into asset-backed finance

Tokenization platform Securitize climbed about 10% postmarket after reporting third quarter growth alongside increased operating expenses, and announcing an expansion into asset-backed finance through a strategic acquisition.

Miami-based Securitize, which made its public market debut in July, operates in the relatively new corner of the crypto equity universe involved in institutional tokenization infrastructure. Tokenized assets on the platform grew 9% in the third quarter, to $4.8 billion, from the same period a year ago. Aggregate transaction volume rose 20% from a year ago, to $4.3 billion. Revenue of $11.5 million was up 28% from last year.

The growth came as bullish sentiment around tokenization gained traction throughout the third quarter, ahead of the Security and Exchange Commission formalizing the long awaited Innovation Exemption on Sept. 17, which created a temporary pathway for trading of certain tokenized stocks and strengthened the regulatory outlook.

Profitability is still meaningfully below year-ago levels, due in part to costs associated with going public over the summer, as well as the scaling up of the tokenization business at an active time for the entire sector, Securitize CEO Carlos Domingo told CNBC in an exclusive interview.

Securitize reinforced "certain areas of the company like finance, compliance, legal [and] cybersecurity," which increased corporate costs "compared to a year ago when we were privately held," Domingo said. "We're in in a very good position. The adjusted EBITDA … is improving significantly from previous quarters."

Securitize reported an adjusted EBITDA loss of $2 million, compared with positive adjusted EBITDA of $3.6 million a year ago, better than a $5.5 million loss in the second quarter. The company posted a 183% increase in operating costs and expenses.

Also on Monday, the Securitize board authorized a $50 million share buyback and announced the acquisition of River One, an institutional asset-backed financing platform developing technology to connect stablecoin capital with traditional lending markets. Terms were not disclosed.

Securitize sees an opportunity to modernize the $9 trillion market for asset backed finance by using stablecoins to finance real world assets, generate yield and tokenize investment positions and credit products, Domingo said.The acquisition also marks an expansion beyond Securitize's core niche markets at a time when tokenization's competitive landscape is growing, with traditional asset managers like BlackRock and Franklin Templeton, and established financial market infrastructure providers like DTCC and Broadridge, entering the industry.

"We have been very active in tokenized funds, treasuries [and] yield … And we've been obviously recently very active in tokenized stocks, so this adds another piece of the puzzle in the emerging tokenization space for us to capture revenue growth."

#133. Honda to invest $3 billion in new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio

MARYSVILLE, Ohio — Honda Motor plans to invest $3 billion to build a new vehicle assembly plant in Ohio, the automaker announced Monday.

Honda said the new plant, which will be near its other facilities in Marysville, Ohio, is expected to begin production in 2029, have a capacity for 240,000 vehicles annually and create more than 2,000 jobs.

The facility is expected to be capable of producing traditional vehicles with internal combustion engines, all-electric vehicles and hybrid models, which have been growing in popularity in the U.S.

Honda declined to disclose what vehicles the company plans to produce at the new facility.

The plant is expected to increase Honda's light-duty vehicle capacity to 1.3 million units annually in the U.S. It currently has 12 manufacturing plants in United States, eight of which produce engines or light-duty vehicles for the auto industry.

"This is not simply an investment in another plant. It is an investment in the future of Honda manufacturing in America," Bob Nelson, executive vice president of American Honda Motor, said during the announcement at the company's U.S. heritage center in Ohio.

The auto industry has been trying to localize more production amid President Donald Trump's tariffs and inflated logistics costs that have cut into profits.

Nelson told CNBC after the event that tariffs are affecting the company's business but the new plant is more about Honda's ambitions to grow in the U.S.

"Tariffs are an impact to us, even with the high localization rate that we have," he said. "But the real impact is that we have customers out there wanting Honda product and we can't give it to them. That's the real area."

Nelson said Honda did not work with the Trump administration ahead of Monday's announcement.

Honda's plans for a new plant come roughly two weeks after Volkswagen-owned luxury brand Audi announced it would invest more than $4 billion in a new plant at a rally with President Donald Trump, which Audi CEO Gernot Dollner faced criticism for attending.

Honda's new plant is the company's first new mass production U.S. vehicle assembly plant since it opened a facility in Indiana in 2008. It also opened a $70 million performance vehicle facility in 2016 and a multibillion-dollar joint venture battery plant earlier this year, both in Ohio.

The announcement comes after the automaker in May indefinitely suspended an $11 billion investment plan to produce all-electric vehicles and batteries in Canada. It also canceled three planned EVs for the U.S. market.

American Honda Motor Co. executive Jennifer Thomas recently emphasized the Japanese automaker's commitment to the U.S. and need for additional capacity.

"In order to be competitive in this market, you're going to need to build products here," Thomas, senior vice president of corporate affairs, said Oct. 27 in Detroit. "We are focused on trying to maximize the existing capacity that we have, but we are at capacity, and so we're exploring all options."

Thomas also warned the automotive industry faces a "catastrophic" scenario if the U.S. and Canada cannot reach a new trade deal by the end of this year, when Trump has threatened to double tariffs on Canadian vehicles and parts from 25% to 50% on Jan. 1 if the two countries don't reach a deal.

Honda said it has built more than 33 million vehicles and more than 39 million engines in the U.S. since it started auto production in the U.S. in 1982.

The Japanese automaker currently has more than 30,000 employees in the U.S.

#132. What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Watch: Who is suspected Chinese spy at centre of Nvidia affair investigation?

One of the Artificial Intelligence (AI) industry's most important figures has been accused of having an affair with a suspected Chinese spy, prompting an investigation.

US intelligence officials believed that Chinese businesswoman Alice Huang, who had a close relationship with Nvidia CEO and co-founder Jensen Huang, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report.

On Sunday, Huang admitted, external to having been "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

US tech giant Nvidia, one of the most valuable companies in the world, has opened an independent investigation into the reports. The firm said it does not believe US national security had been compromised - and added that Huang was committed to continuing to lead the company.

President Donald Trump said on Monday that the FBI is also investigating the affair.

The FBI declined to comment further when contacted by the BBC.

Trump said he spoke with Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Here is what we know about the affair and why it matters.

Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Jensen Huang (left) and Alice Huang - also known as Huang Le - pictured at an event

US officials believed that Alice Huang had been recruited to spy by China's top intelligence agency, the New York Times reported, external. Her current whereabouts are unknown.

Alice Huang, who is of no relation to Jensen Huang, was a top executive at Singapore-based data centre firm Megaspeed between March and November 2023. The firm had been suspected of helping divert Nvidia chips to China, a concern that Nvidia maintains was investigated and debunked.

However, the BBC understands Megaspeed is under investigation by the Singapore government regarding previous allegations that the company was circumventing US sanctions on the export of Nvidia's high-end chips. The BBC has contacted Megaspeed for comment.

The company told the New York Times last month its current leadership took the allegations seriously and had appointed external legal advisers to conduct an internal investigation.

Surveillance reportedly revealed communications between Alice Huang and an American citizen, who was subsequently identified as Jensen Huang through a process known as "unmasking".

It remains unclear what, if any, intelligence Alice Huang â who is also known as Huang Le â allegedly gathered and passed to Beijing. She did not respond to the paper's request for comment.

When the BBC asked Nvidia if the company was aware of intelligence concerns, the company said it was not.

"We trust that if we were the target of a genuine threat, the US government would have notified us," an Nvidia spokesperson told the BBC.

China has rejected the spying allegations.

Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun told reporters on Monday at a regular daily press briefing: "We have already responded to this. The allegations about Chinese spies are entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China."

The New York Times reported in late October that Trump had been briefed last year on intelligence alleging Alice Huang was spying on China's behalf.

Asked by reporters on Sunday whether he wished he had "acted on the intelligence", Trump responded: "No, I don't think so. I think he's an honourable guy."

US Secretary of State Marco Rubio, meanwhile, refused to comment when asked about Huang. Last spring, the secretary of state reportedly oversaw the drafting of a memo that warned the Trump administration about Huang's relationship with the Chinese businesswoman, according to the New York Times.

Nvidia shares slide as US tech giant investigates CEO's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Why Trump is all-in on AI despite the warnings

What is AI, how does it work and why are some people concerned about it?

Nvidia is at the heart of US and China's AI race, which is why the billionaire's affair has sparked national security fears among American lawmakers.

Trump has said "whoever wins AI wins", and the fear is that if China gains the upper hand in AI, it will supercharge the country's military and surveillance capabilities.

The US advantage is in large part because of US companies like Nvidia, working with producers like the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC), which makes more than 90% of the world's most advanced chips.

Since 2022, the US has used various export controls to keep the top tech out of China, which it says helps retain America's competitive edge and protects its national security.

This means companies like Nvidia have been largely locked out of the world's most lucrative market, with Huang once saying that Nvidia's share of the Chinese market had gone from around 95% to effectively zero.

Huang has engaged in shuttle diplomacy between Washington and Beijing, hoping to find a US-approved way back in, and shaping US policy on China along the way.

Sunday's revelations may explain why Huang has failed to bring home the biggest prize of all to Nvidia: the Trump administration's blessing to sell all but its most advanced chips to China.

Huang has soared to international importance with his company

Born in Taiwan in 1963, Huang was nine years old when his family moved to the West Coast of the US. He graduated as an electrical engineer from Oregon State University, and then with a master's degree in the same field from Stanford University.

Huang then worked at US chip firm AMD before co-founding Nvidia. Known as "Daddy Huang" in Taiwan and once lauded as the "Taylor Swift of tech", he has a reputation as a workaholic and perfectionist with incredibly high standards for his staff.

He has an estimated fortune of $155bn (Â£115bn), according to the Bloomberg Billionaires Index.

"Jensen Huang is a tech visionary of the likes of Steve Jobs," Stephen Witt, the author of Thinking Machine, a biography on Huang, told the BBC, adding that he was responsible for creating AI hardware as we know it today.

Huang has a famously close relationship with Trump. At the recent AI summit at the White House, where 42 tech executives were present, the president was flanked by Elon Musk and Huang.

Trump said he had not spoken to the Nvidia boss since news of the investigation emerged.

"I just know he's a fine person, but you never know what's going on," Trump told reporters, adding that he had "done an amazing job for our country".

There is no clear successor for Huang at Nvidia

Founded by Huang and fellow electrical engineers Chris Malachowsky and Curtis Priem in 1993, the company was originally known for chips that process graphics, particularly for computer games.

Eventually it changed focus to AI, a field it now dominates.

Huang rescued Nvidia from bankruptcy on more than one occasion, and took risks on AI a long time before the rest of the world did, effectively building the infrastructure from scratch.

"Today when we think of that, we think of these giant data centres - none of that existed. There were no customers for the stuff he was building. In fact his first customer was Open AI at the time they had 30 people - he actually gave it to them, he donated it to them," Witt told the BBC.

Every notable tech company building AI tools and infrastructure, including Amazon, Meta, Google, and Microsoft, now use Nvidia chips.

Currently worth $4.5tn (Â£3.4tn), Nvidia is one of the most valuable companies ever.

For now, the firm's board appears to have little appetite for removing Huang, saying in a statement on Sunday that they have "confidence in Jensen and his leadership team".

Sources tell the BBC his removal is unlikely because there is so much money at stake.

There is no public succession plan for the 63-year-old. He has often said he hopes to continue in the role for decades, and Bloomberg reports he has created an informal leadership structure around him unlike a traditional company's hierarchy.

His children, Spencer and Madison, and son-in-law Nico Caprez work at Nvidia in senior roles.

Lori Huang accompanied her husband at the state dinner held by President Donald Trump for his Chinese counterpart Xi Jinping in September

Huang met his future wife Lori Mills at Oregon State University while studying electrical engineering in the early 1980s. The pair were lab partners.

They married in 1985, about eight years before he co-founded Nvidia, and have two children, Madison and Spencer.

In 2007, the couple founded the Jen-Hsun and Lori Huang Foundation. The charitable fund has seen its assets surge in recent years, driven by the soaring value of Nvidia shares, and is estimated to be worth more than $9bn.

Its donations include tens of millions of dollars to engineering programmes at Oregon State and Stanford University.

Witt told the BBC that Huang has gone to great lengths to present himself as a family man, adding: "That's very important to him".

"He praises his wife, he talks about her in very glowing ways," he added. "The fallout of this would be bad for anyone's family. But for someone who has gone to such great lengths to cultivate an image of being a family guy and who keeps his children very close, it is very bad."

Additional reporting by Tom McArthur in London, Ayeshea Perera and Tessa Wong in Singapore and Helen Davidson in Sydney

#129. Poundland unable to buy new stock as fears over collapse grow

Poundland is unable to buy new stock to fill its shelves after being blocked by its owners from accessing cash in its bank accounts.

The discount chain is on the brink of collapse with one insider describing it as "running on fumes". It has until Thursday to secure a rescue deal.

About Â£50m has been frozen by its American owner Gordon Brothers, the BBC understands, meaning its 600 shops are running out of stock.

Pressure is building on Gordon Brothers, which bought Poundland for Â£1 last June, after the owner rejected a takeover bid that could have saved the thousands of jobs currently at risk. Gordon Brothers has been approached for comment.

Poundland has filed a notice of intention to appoint administrators, which protects it from its creditors until Thursday.

Access to funds was limited because of a tension between Gordon Brothers' dual role as both creditor and shareholder, a source said, but added they expected a relaxation of funds in the next two days.

Last week, Poundland boss Barry Williams told staff like-for-like sales, which exclude new store openings, were up 11.4% last week, which included Halloween, but said this was Â£1.5m ahead of budget.

He said the business made a profit of Â£18.1m in the year to September, which was a "strong recovery" from the previous year where the business made losses of Â£68m.

The latest annual accounts have yet to be audited.

Burnham 'ready to support' Poundland staff as retailer faces collapse

Poundland staff protest after chain approach administrators

However, Gordon Brothers has painted a different picture of the business, saying the chain remains "challenged and vulnerable to any potential downturn in future trading".

It said it had provided significant funding to support the company's ability to trade since it acquired the business.

It has also made Â£18m, from a combination of debt interest payments and other fees and charges.

On Wednesday, executives from Gordon Brothers will appear before the Business Select Committee with MPs probing whether they are genuinely pursuing a solvent rescue of the business, which has 11,000 employees, and whether every realistic step is being taken to protect jobs and stores.

The session is being held in private given commercially sensitive discussions are still under way with bidders.

On Thursday, the 10-day moratorium to protect Poundland from its creditors expires, but if there is a prospect of a solvent deal, the deadline could be extended.

If not, Poundland is set to collapse into administration and could be broken up.

A source familiar with the sales process said London-based Xyra Group was making "significant progress" in its last minute bid to buy Poundland and could now be the front runner.

An extension of Thursday's deadline now seems an increasingly likely scenario. But such a case would be difficult to put forward if Gordon Brothers continues to block funding.

One observer remarked "if Gordon Brothers is going in front of that committee, you don't want to be giving the message we're pulling up the drawbridge".

Even if a deal is agreed to sell the business as a whole, there is no guarantee a new owner would not reduce the number of stores, but suppliers to Poundland would prefer that outcome to hundreds of sites being sold off to other retailers.

There has been interest from the likes of Poundstretcher, Tesco and B&M in picking up stores which could mean continuing employment for many workers.

The central issue is whether a piecemeal sale would protect more jobs than a buyer willing to keep Poundland trading as a national chain.

#126. Goldman CEO Solomon says AI financing remains strong despite market jitters

Goldman Sachs CEO David Solomon told CNBC on Monday that financing demand for artificial intelligence infrastructure remains strong despite recent market jitters as investors reassess the risks involving the massive build-out.

Solomon said that his firm has been "very active" in AI capital markets deals over recent weeks, a setup that he expects to continue.

"There's still very strong demand for investments in the AI infrastructure build and the technology around the growth of AI," Solomon told CNBC's Leslie Picker.

Changes in the political environment are among factors that will "create recalibrations and ups and downs, and we're seeing some of that," Solomon said. "But I still deeply believe in the long-term trajectory of the power of AI in our economy to drive productivity."

AI-related stocks, including chipmakers and neocloud providers, have tumbled in recent weeks as investors reassess whether the hundreds of billions of dollars being poured into AI infrastructure will generate good returns.

The selloff, which began before the U.S. midterm elections, intensified after Democrats scored sweeping victories on Tuesday, raising concerns that political opposition could slow the industry's expansion.

The shifting sentiment comes at a key moment for AI model lab Anthropic, which is preparing for a potential IPO that could become one of the biggest tests yet of public-market appetite for AI companies. Goldman is among the banks leading that IPO process.

"We'll see, obviously, if this IPO moves forward, how it all plays out," Solomon said, declining to comment in detail about the IPO. "I would say that the market environment is still pretty constructive."

Solomon also pushed back against political resistance to data center construction, arguing that the infrastructure is essential to the U.S. economy and that it can be built responsibly.

"We certainly don't want this infrastructure going to places outside the U.S., which is what will naturally happen because the demand for compute is there," Solomon said.

#123. How a civil servant got away with juggling five jobs for so long

Kashim Chowdhury fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money

Aleesha Adams joined a video call with Kashim Chowdhury in May 2023.

He'd been on sick leave from his job at the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA) for nearly three months and Adams, his line manager, wanted to put a plan in action to ease him back into work.

At the start of the call, Chowdhury was coughing and waving tissues up to the camera in an attempt to show how ill he was. But what really caught Adams' attention was how pale he looked.

She noticed white powder had started separating from his forehead. "He was sweating, it was like rubbing off, so it was darker underneath and darker on the neck," she told his trial.

She claimed he was wearing white make-up to look sick. Something he denied in court.

Little did she know UKHSA was not Chowdhury's only employer.

That month, he was also being paid to work full-time for the Department for the Environment, Food and Rural Affairs (Defra), the Department for Business and Trade, the Food Standards Agency and the Home Office. He was contracted to work 183 hours a week â more than the number of hours in seven days. He fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money.

In total, he had eight overlapping full-time public-sector jobs between 2020 and 2023. During that time, this relatively junior civil servant was paid Â£350,000.

Soon after her call with Chowdhury, Adams discovered that he had logged into the call using a Defra email address. When she challenged him about it, he blamed it on a technical error. She told the jury at his trial at Southwark Crown Court: "I believed he was lying." She escalated her concerns up the chain.

But his fraudulent behaviour remained undetected. Chowdhury was able to keep working multiple government jobs until September 2023 when he left the Civil Service.

He was finally caught in 2024 by the government's National Fraud Initiative, which conducts searches of Civil Service databases, such as payroll, every two years to look for anomalies.

On Monday he was found guilty of nine counts of fraud and told by the judge that he would be facing a "very substantial custodial sentence".

Chowdhury, 56, arrived each day at court alone. He is married, yet there never seemed to be any friends or family there to support him.

He would often make his way down the corridors of the court building carrying two plastic carrier bags, with a bobble hat perched on his head.

He sometimes looked bewildered in the witness box.

Chowdhury, who had a diagnosis of PTSD in 2015, said that he had taken on so many roles because he feared losing his livelihood after experiencing insecure employment. He said he'd been forced out of a job at the Department for Education in 2013.

He said he hadn't been thinking clearly when applying for jobs ''because of my stress, distress and trauma and due to my panic at the time".

But the prosecution argued his behaviour was "deliberate" and "intentional dishonesty" and that he had exploited the system.

Chowdhury sometimes looked bewildered in the witness box

However, the case raises important questions for the Civil Service. How was he able to get away with it for so long?

Part of the explanation is that the Civil Service's human resources system is not centralised. Chowdhury did not declare on government application forms that he was already a civil servant, so the system did not spot the duplication.

Hannah Keenan, who works at the Institute for Government, says the system clearly failed. "Government departments are taking hiring decisions independently of each other and without systems that join up," she said.

"That the model and the culture surrounding it aren't working shouldn't be a surprise though - we've known for a long time about poor performers flying under the radar by shuffling between jobs."

There are more than 550,000 people employed across the Civil Service, many in offices in Whitehall, in central London. They are responsible for carrying out government policy and the daily administration of the country.

Another reason why Chowdhury's fraud went undetected is that he was working entirely from the east London house he shared with his mother. In 2023, she had dementia and terminal cancer, and he told his employers that he needed to be at home to look after her.

But rather than reducing his workload to concentrate on her care, he took on more jobs. He covered up his impossible daily schedule by getting sick notes from his GP.

In April 2023, he was off with what he called "mixed anxiety and depressive disorder" from Defra, the UKHSA, the Department for Business and Trade and the Food Standards Agency.

While the sick notes were shown to the jury, they were not given evidence about what caused the depressive disorder or of a psychiatric assessment having been done during this period.

His sickness did not prevent him from starting yet another job â this time at the Home Office. He did not mention to them he was ill, nor did he tell them he was working elsewhere.

Unsurprisingly, a succession of his line managers told the jury he had not been very good at his jobs.

Especially damning was the evidence given by Dan McCartney, who managed Chowdhury at Defra. He said he had to "effectively tell him what to do the whole time" and that Chowdhury kept his camera off during video calls for four months, claiming technical problems.

But because Chowdhury was not subject to any formal performance measures at Defra, he was given a permanent job. He remained there for two years.

In September 2023, Chowdhury was found to have more than Â£40,000 in his bank accounts as well as significant investments, including Â£45,000 in premium bonds.

A recent survey of 1,700 civil servants by the independent think tank Re:State found 76% of respondents thought it did not do a good job of tackling poor performers.

An ex-senior civil servant told the BBC that much of the Civil Service is culturally averse to performance management.

"Whitehall's known for ages it has to be less risk averse. Shrinking the Civil Service and the adoption of artificial intelligence technology make that more vital," they said.

"But don't underestimate the fear factor: fear of drawn-out grievances and fear of being accused of wasting taxpayers' money if the Civil Service was to do what big private sector firms do, which is cut to get rid of poor performers by offering some money and a settlement agreement."

A Defra spokesperson said: "Kashim Chowdhury abused the trust placed in him as a public servant and defrauded taxpayers out of almost Â£285,000. He will now rightly face the consequences for his actions.

"This conviction is the result of a thorough and painstaking investigation led by the Defra Counter Fraud and Investigations team. It sends a clear message that fraud against the public purse will not be tolerated and those responsible will be held to account."

The spokesperson said Defra would use "all available avenues to recover the proceeds of Chowdhury's offending on behalf of taxpayers".

Chowdhury's case also illustrates a wider issue.

He was engaged in what is being called "polygamous working", where people hold down several jobs secretly. Websites have even been set up offering people advice on how to keep their employers in the dark.

Its rise has been blamed on larger numbers of people working from home.

The government's National Fraud Initiative has identified 23 cases of "polygamous working" across London in its most recent report. It uncovered Â£500,000 of overpaid salaries over a period of a few months.

Alongside the fraud case, Chowdhury is involved in a legal dispute with his three siblings, who accuse him of ''bullying'' their mother into signing over half of her Â£2.1m home to him in the final days of her life. He denies it.

At times during his criminal trial, Chowdhury appeared confused by what he was hearing and blamed trauma for the web of lies he had spun. The jury took seven hours to reach their verdict and decided he was motivated by personal gain - stealing tens of thousands of pounds from the taxpayer.

Chowdhury now faces a significant custodial sentence.

Civil servant who secretly held five jobs found guilty of fraud
