#120. Nvidia’s Trump ties draw scrutiny after reports Huang was targeted by Chinese intelligence

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang has spent the past two years cultivating an unparalleled relationship with President Donald Trump, gaining extraordinary access and influence over federal artificial intelligence policy as his company has pushed for looser restrictions on selling advanced chips to China.

Now, revelations about Huang's affair with a suspected Chinese spy are putting that carefully built alliance under scrutiny — along with the millions of dollars Huang and other Nvidia executives have spent expanding the company's political influence in Washington.

The chipmaker is nearly inseparable from its co-founder and CEO Huang, who during Trump's second term has gone from being nearly unknown to the president to being part of a small cadre of executives accompanying him on Air Force One.

Nvidia has committed at least $26 million to Trump's pet projects, including $25 million toward his White House ballroom and $1 million to his 2025 inaugural fund. The company initially sought to keep its ballroom contribution anonymous, according to documents obtained by The Washington Post. Its ballroom pledge was tied with Apple's for the largest disclosed corporate contribution, the Post reported.

Those ties are drawing renewed scrutiny from congressional Democrats, including Sen. Richard Blumenthal, D-Conn., who pressed Huang in November 2025 about Nvidia's ballroom donation and White House contacts. Blumenthal re-upped his warning on Thursday, while expected incoming House oversight Chairman Robert Garcia, D-Calif., vowed to investigate corporate corruption when Democrats take control in January.

Trump continued to defend Huang on Sunday, calling him an "honorable guy" and saying he had heard nothing suggesting wrongdoing. The president was briefed about the suspected Chinese intelligence operation in October 2025 but chose not to act, according to The New York Times. Secretary of State Marco Rubio oversaw a classified memo detailing the concerns this spring, the Times reported.

The president's defense underscores how Huang has become one of Trump's most prominent allies in the technology industry, repeatedly praising the president, attending White House events and accompanying him on a trip to China in May. Trump is due to return to China next week for a summit with other world leaders.

Congressional scrutiny on Huang ramped up after the Times reported Oct. 29 that U.S. intelligence determined Alice Huang, a Chinese citizen who is an executive at Singapore-based cloud computing company Megaspeed, was funneling information to Beijing and had “developed a close relationship” with Jensen Huang. The two Huangs are unrelated.

House Republican committee leaders on Thursday asked Jensen Huang to sit for a closed-door briefing on his ties with Alice Huang, saying recent reports "raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's access to sensitive U.S. technology and information."

In a statement to his company on Sunday, Jensen Huang admitted to the affair and apologized. He also revealed that Nvidia's board of directors has "initiated an independent investigation," which he said he supports and will cooperate with.

"I love our country and our company. I am committed to protecting NVIDIA and supporting America's national security. I am proud and grateful to be an American," the CEO said.

Nvidia didn't immediately respond to CNBC's request for comment.

House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries, D-N.Y., told CBS News on Monday morning that "high-level" Trump administration officials would lead a classified briefing for lawmakers later in the day. An administration official, speaking on condition of anonymity to discuss the private meeting, told CNBC U.S. intelligence chiefs John Ratcliffe and Jay Clayton were previously scheduled to lead a classified briefing on threats related to Iran with top congressional leaders.

Later Monday morning, the Times reported that the Commerce Department was investigating Megaspeed until August 2025, when the probe was abruptly transferred to the Department of Justice following outreach from Nvidia's general counsel about the matter.

Thomas Wackman, spokesman for Commerce's Bureau of Industry and Security, told CNBC in a statement, "BIS conducts all enforcement strictly by the book. BIS enforces aggressively across the board, including with respect to chip smuggling, and enforcement is at an all-time high." The DOJ did not respond to CNBC's request for comment.

"NVIDIA's CEO just confirmed a relationship with a suspected Chinese spy," Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the top Democrat on the Senate Banking Committee who is expected to chair the panel next year, posted on X Monday morning. "Congress is back today. We have legislation ready to go to stop advanced AI chips from fueling China's military."

Huang's access to Trump has coincided with a series of policy reversals that eased restrictions on Nvidia's chip sales to China, a lucrative market the company has fought to preserve.

In April 2025, Huang attended a $1 million-per-person dinner at Mar-a-Lago, after which the administration briefly held off on restricting Nvidia's H20 chips before imposing the limits days later. Following another meeting with Trump in July, the administration reversed course and began approving sales to China.

In December 2025, days after another meeting with Huang, Trump announced plans to permit exports of Nvidia's more powerful H200 chips to approved Chinese customers. The Commerce Department formally relaxed its licensing restrictions the following month.

Huang kept up the pressure in 2026, accompanying Trump on a trip to China as Nvidia fought congressional efforts to tighten export controls.

But Nvidia's success in Washington has yet to translate into unfettered access to China. Beijing has withheld approval for large-scale H200 imports, leaving hundreds of thousands of chips awaiting shipment as of early November.

Nvidia has also quietly ramped up its Washington influence operation, spending a record $2.15 million on federal lobbying in the third quarter — up 72% from the previous quarter and 10% from a year earlier, according to company disclosures. Its $4.69 million in 2026 lobbying expenses through September nearly matches the $4.97 million it spent in all of 2025.

The company's in-house lobbyists include alumni of congressional offices from key Republicans and Democrats, including Rep. Michael McCaul, R-Texas, who previously chaired the House Foreign Affairs Committee.

In June, Nvidia hired former Obama Commerce Department official and Intel lobbying chief Bruce Andrews to lead its Washington operation, then launched its first employee-funded PAC in August. The PAC raised more than $263,000 through September but had yet to back any candidates, according to a CNBC analysis of Federal Election Commission filings. Its next disclosure is due Dec. 3. Separately, two Nvidia executives — Gavin Sherry, vice president of software, and Sundeep Madra, vice president of hardware — each contributed $443,000 to the Republican National Committee on June 24.

The donations help illustrate the depth of Huang and Trump's relationship as the $4.3 trillion tech behemoth Nvidia buoys the U.S. economy and lends crucial advantage over China in the race for AI dominance.

They also raise further questions about the Trump administration's response to U.S. intelligence reportedly revealing that the loyal and powerful Nvidia CEO had a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

"Obviously Nvidia needs to take significant steps in its operations, security, and policy advocacy to ensure it takes national security concerns more seriously," Chris McGuire, a senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council on Foreign Relations, said in an X post Monday. "But it is even more important that the Department of Commerce tighten up export controls to prevent things like this from ever happening again.

"It is utterly baffling that despite being privy to this information for months, they have not taken even the most basic actions to protect the country, such as cutting off a company that is literally run by Chinese spies from buying AI chips," McGuire wrote.

#119. Civil servant who secretly held five jobs found guilty of fraud

Civil servant Kashim Chowdhury juggled multiple public sector jobs

A civil servant who secretly held multiple public sector jobs on full-time salaries has been found guilty of nine counts of fraud.

Kashim Chowdhury, from east London, had five government jobs that overlapped in the spring of 2023. He fraudulently earned more than Â£284,000 of taxpayers' money.

The prosecution accused the 56-year-old of ''deliberate, intentional dishonesty'' and of ''exploiting the system''.

After seven hours of deliberation, the jury unanimously found Chowdhury guilty of eight counts of fraud by false representation and one count of fraud by a failure to disclose.

The judge said Chowdhury faced a ''very significant custodial sentence''. He added he was refusing bail because of the significance of the crime and for Chowdhury's own wellbeing.

"This was sustained and deliberate dishonesty," said Sarah Melo, specialist prosecutor at the CPS Serious Economic Organised Crime and International Directorate.

"He knew he could not legitimately hold multiple full-time positions at the same time, but continued to conceal what he was doing."

A Defra spokesperson said Chowdury "abused the trust placed in him as a public servant", adding that it would use all available avenues to recover the proceeds of Chowdhury's offending.

The judge said Chowdhury faced a ''very significant custodial sentence''

Between 2020 and 2023 Chowdhury had eight full-time public sector roles that overlapped and earned him Â£350,000 in total.

The prosecution told the court that on application forms he provided inaccurate information about his employment status and failed to disclose to government departments that he was already a civil servant. Once he secured the roles he didn't tell his employers he was working elsewhere.

He was allowed to work entirely from home because he told his employers he was caring for his mother, whom he lived with in east London. She had dementia and was diagnosed with terminal cancer in 2023.

How a civil servant got away with juggling five jobs for so long

But rather than reduce his workload to look after her, he kept taking on new jobs. In the spring of 2023, when his mother was a few months away from death, he had jobs at the Department for Environment, Food and Rural Affairs, the Department of Health and Social Care, the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA), the Department for Business and Trade, the Food Standards Agency and the Home Office.

During this period he was contracted to work 183 hours a week - more than the number of hours that exist in seven days.

Chowdhury said he had taken on so many roles because he feared losing his livelihood after experiencing insecure employment in the past. He was made redundant from a job at the Department for Education in 2013 and was given a diagnosis of PTSD.

He said he had not been thinking clearly when applying for jobs ''because of my stress, distress and trauma and due to my panic at the time".

But the prosecution barrister, Lucy Organ, told the jury: "He's lied to you in evidence, he's lied to you under oath, which demonstrates the level of deceit he has descended to."

A succession of his managers told the court about his poor performance. Especially damning was the evidence given by Dan McCartney who managed Chowdhury at Defra.

He said he had to "effectively tell him what to do the whole time". McCartney said that during video calls Chowdhury had his camera switched off blaming technical problems. McCartney said this went on for four to five months.

But McCartney said that because Chowdhury was not subject to any formal performance measures at Defra, he was given a permanent job and remained there for two years.

Chowdhury told the jury he had not worn make-up during a work video call

The prosecution argued that he used sick notes to cope with his impossible schedule. In March 2023, he was on sick leave from four of the organisations he worked for. But he kept working at the Home Office and did not tell them he was ill.

His line manager at the UK Health Security Agency (UKHSA), Aleesha Adams, organised a video call with him in May 2023. By this point he'd been off sick for three months and she wanted to make a plan to get him back to work.

She told the jury that at the start of the call he was coughing a lot and waving tissues up to the camera. She also told the jury that he had been wearing white make-up to try to look ill.

Adams told the court that as the meeting went on the make-up started coming off his forehead. ''He was sweating, it was like rubbing off so it was darker underneath and darker on the neck."

Chowdhury told the jury this was a smear and that he had not been wearing make-up.

After the call, Adams discovered that he had logged into the call using a Defra email address. When she challenged him about it he blamed a technical error.

Adams grew suspicious. ''I believed he was lying,'' she told the jury. She escalated her concerns up the chain at the UKHSA.

But his fraudulent behaviour went on for several more months until he left the civil service in September 2023.

He was finally caught in 2024 by the government's National Fraud Initiative, which conducts searches of civil service databases such as payroll every two years to look for anomalies.

Chowdhury has also been accused by his siblings of bullying their mother into increasing his share of the family home days before she died.

In a case at the High Court, Chowdhury's two brothers and sister allege that he put "undue influence" on Faijun Nessa to sign documents that meant he would inherit half of her home.

The four-storey, six-bedroom home in east London has been valued at Â£2.1m.

His siblings say their mother could not have understood what she was signing because she was "desperately unwell". In January 2023 she was given a terminal diagnosis of stage four metastatic colorectal cancer.

The claimants also allege that Mrs Nessa, who was nearly blind and could not speak English, was unaware of what she was signing because the documents were not translated into her native Bengali at the time they were signed.

Kashim Chowdhury denies the claims. That case is ongoing.

#118. Boeing to cut 5% of commercial airplane workers as production increase takes longer than expected

Boeing said Monday that it plans to cut 5% of its commercial airplane workforce as its production ramp-up is moving at a slower pace than the company expected.

The cuts will be through a combination of attrition and layoffs, Stephanie Pope, head of the commercial aircraft unit, said in a memo to staff.

Pope reiterated that the company is still working to stabilize production rates of its bestselling 737 Max planes, which are made outside of Seattle.

CEO Kelly Ortberg told investors last month that the company won't significantly increase production of the Max aircraft until the second half of next year. He said the company's wing production has been slower than expected.

"Adjusting our production schedules and reducing aircraft output puts us in an overstaffed position," Pope said in her note Monday. "Unfortunately, that means we need to align our staffing levels to our revised business plan, resulting in a reduction of approximately 5% through a combination of attrition and involuntary layoffs, with notifications beginning in December."

Boeing is targeting a production rate of 52 planes a month in the second half of 2027. Ortberg said the company has been trying to stabilize output at a rate of about 47 of its 737 Max planes a month.

#115. Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Jensen Huang, 63, has been called the 'Taylor Swift of tech' in the US

The tech world has spent the day digesting the extraordinary news that Jensen Huang - the charismatic, leather-jacket wearing chief executive of the multi-trillion dollar firm Nvidia - is being investigated by his own company over an extramarital affair with a woman alleged to be a Chinese spy.

None of us had that on our bingo cards for 2026.

Huang's alleged romantic affair with Alice Huang (no relation), the former CEO of a Singapore-based data centre company, has cast ripples far beyond his immediate family - undoubtedly on an emotional rollercoaster of their own - taking us to a crossroads at which technology, geopolitics and national security intersect.

In a nutshell, this is about the bitter rivalry between the US and China, as well as whether Jensen Huang has betrayed the US as well as his wife.

The US is locked in a fierce race with China to develop the best AI models.

"Whoever wins AI, wins," as US President Donald Trump puts it. He thinks that the country with the more dominant AI technology will benefit the most from it and, ultimately, will be the most powerful in the world. He may well be right.

Nvidia happens to make the best chips in the business for AI â so much so that it has been overwhelmed with demand.

It sets its prices accordingly: a single unit of the firm's latest kit for data centres starts at Â£37,000 ($50,000). A medium or large site might have hundreds of them.

The US administration initially slapped an export ban on Nvidia's more recent devices to prevent China from taking advantage of their superior capabilities. It has since softened the rules a little.

But there are suggestions that Alice Huang's company, Megaspeed, may have been involved in sneaking them into the country while she was in charge. Bloomberg reported that the firm bought billions of dollars worth of Nvidia tech in November 2025. Megaspeed denies that it was used illegitimately.

For his part, Jensen Huang has lobbied heavily for the export rules between the two countries to be relaxed, arguing against the weight of general opinion that the AI race doesn't matter and that it would be no bad thing for China to become dependent on US tech.

"President Trump wants American to win and President Xi wants China to win, and it's possible for both of them to do so," he told the BBC last year.

It think it's safe to assume that neither country sees it that way.

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

The difficult questions Nvidia now has to try to answer include whether Alice gained special access to the firm's latest technology, either deliberately or not, via Jensen - and, if so, whether she shared it with Beijing or others.

We don't know when their affair began or ended, but there are numerous photos and videos of Jensen and Alice together at events. It is feasible that there were moments when they spent quite a lot of time together and Jensen has talked about always having an eye on his work.

China denies that she was engaged in espionage.

Interest in economic espionage is neither new nor unique to China. But many Western countries say that China does it on a grand scale, using a combination of technology tools and agents embedded in various organisations - including private companies.

China having access to significant enough numbers of Nvidia's chips to make use of them, or stripping them down and learning how to make its own versions, would infuriate the Trump administration, which is trying to keep its AI technology close to its chest.

Some models have even been withheld from the UK-based AI Safety Institute on the grounds that they are potentially too dangerous to be shared. The US does not want anyone having a headstart or misusing its innovations.

Another potential bombshell lies in the form of any national security information to which Jensen Huang may have been privy.

He is a man operating at the very top of the US elite - one of a handful of people who could probably walk into the White House unannounced and have tea with Trump. He has influence, he has access and he has powerful friends. It's quite possible that secrets were shared with him.

Nvidia says there's no evidence so far that he passed anything on and the firm has launched an internal investigation. Huang says he will co-operate and wants to keep his job - though if nothing else, he has broken Nvidia's own code of conduct, which states that company members should not have relationships with clients.

At present, he has the backing of both the Nvidia board and Trump, who described him as "honourable" and speculated that there must have been a reason for what had transpired. In other words, he's not throwing his tech boss buddy under the bus.

Nvidia's share price has fallen but has not tanked spectacularly, suggesting shareholders are not abandoning the company under his stewardship in their droves either.

An AI industry with a weakened Nvidia would undoubtedly be a slower one.

And there isn't really a natural successor waiting in the wings to replace Jensen were he forced out.

Huang has run Nvidia for more than 30 years - to many, he is Nvidia. He has steered it through multiple crises and it was his decision for the firm to focus on AI before it was trendy.

He is known to be a demanding boss who expects staff to work long hours and to high standards. There are stories about him calling all-staff meetings to tell off individuals who had made mistakes. He has said this was to prevent others making the same errors â but you can only imagine how it felt for those on the receiving end.

While he awaits the conclusion of the investigation into his actions, Huang must hope that, despite not tolerating the mistakes of his colleagues in the past, they will be gracious about his own failings.

And he must also live with the irony that he was potentially caught out not by a sophisticated high-tech sting, but by the oldest trick in the book: a honey trap.

#114. CNBC's The China Connection newsletter: Wall Street dips in again. What needs to go right

Hi, this is Evelyn, writing to you from Beijing. Welcome to the latest edition of The China Connection — a snapshot of what I'm seeing and hearing from local businesses.

China has long wanted global investors' money to bolster the prominence of its domestic stock market. Giving foreigners that access hasn't happened overnight. Why are Wall Street firms still trying?

Global investment firms are eyeing mainland China not just for its tech stocks, but for the millions of affluent people who could become their clients.

Mainland Chinese with more than $125,000 in bankable assets hold a combined $27 trillion in wealth, according to financial analytics firm Crisil Coalition Greenwich. And the millionaire population has surged — to nearly 4 million, up from 1 million a decade ago.

On the heels of getting Beijing's approval to establish a wholly-owned securities company, Citi's CEO Jane Fraser is kicking off the U.S. bank's annual China conference this week in Shanghai. It's the latest U.S. financial firm to benefit from China's pledge after the Trump-Xi summit in September to approve applications from all foreign financial institutions to operate in the country.

Also headlining Citi's China conference in person is Anna Wintour, former editor-in-chief of Vogue. Condé Nast did not respond to a request for comment.

"The environment is meaningfully more open than it was a decade ago, although it remains highly regulated," said Nuno Cordeiro, head of wealth management, and Fabio Zheng, senior product manager, wealth management, at Crisil Coalition Greenwich.

They pointed out that compared to past years, the property market slowdown has also created greater demand for diversification and advisory services.

"The question today is no longer whether foreign firms can participate. It is whether they can generate sufficient scale and returns within those constraints," they said in an email. "Consequently, most successful strategies combine greater local presence with partnerships that provide access to domestic distribution."

Franklin Templeton, which oversees $1.8 trillion in assets, and ChinaAMC, which manages over 3.1 trillion yuan ($462.5 billion), signed a strategic partnership on Oct. 28 for sharing market insights and jointly developing products.

They are already working on co-branded ETFs across Hong Kong, U.S. and European fund structures.

"Just as a lot of international investors [are] interested in China investment, Chinese investors are looking for global investment vehicles," Sherrie Dai, managing director, global capital, at ChinaAMC, told me.

A Hong Kong-listed ETF launched with Franklin Templeton on Sept. 29 holds around 40% U.S. stocks and 60% Hong Kong-listed ones.

Such products could, in six to 12 months, meet Beijing's threshold for letting mainland China-based investors participate, Dai said. Her company is also launching products for investors in Brazil to allocate to Chinese tech companies.

Since early 2025, DeepSeek's AI breakthroughs and other Chinese tech advances have gained foreign investors' attention. U.S.-based Roundhill Investments included China stocks in its thematic ETFs focusing on humanoids, memory chips or other tech — but exposure to mainland-listed names may sometimes come through derivatives.

Performance hasn't helped. Chinese stocks have tumbled this year, significantly underperforming their U.S. peers.

On the China business front, foreign financial firms have also struggled. In late 2025, BlackRock decided to shut down its three-year-old China Innovation Fund, which only had $31.7 million in net assets. Vanguard in 2023 sold its stake in a mainland China mutual fund operator to majority owner Ant Group.

Peter Alexander, founder and managing director of Z-Ben Advisors in Shanghai, attributed part of the challenge to "micromanagement" from headquarters outside China.

"Global groups have failed in China due solely to their inability to make the necessary adjustments to how it is they operate to best fit the Chinese market," he said.

Capital controls, a state-dominated system and varying levels of investor communication contribute to a mainland Chinese stock market quite different from that of the U.S.

State-linked funds, known as the "National Team," often step up stock purchases to limit market sell-offs.

Last week, amid reports of a pause in new Chinese IPOs, a senior financial regulator warned in a speech that too much capital concentration in tech can drive up leverage, while crowding out traditional industries.

Such headlines can support short-term stock market gains. But for longer-term investors, who might not be able to buy in immediately, rapid price surges raise the risk that they are "buying at the top," said Liqian Ren, a quantitative manager at U.S.-based fund manager WisdomTree.

High volatility means those investors can only hold a certain amount of Chinese stocks due to perceived risk, she said. The market "becomes a trading vehicle. That's not necessarily good for China."

"Ultimately you need the market to figure it out. You need the punishment to come from the market."

China expands overseas mining stakes, tightening grip on critical minerals

Chinese mineral acquisitions abroad in the first three quarters of the year along marked the highest annually since at least 2018, according to Rhodium Group. That adds to the existing rare earths leverage Beijing has used in U.S. trade tensions. China is also demanding lower tariffs before buying more U.S. agricultural goods.

What the Democratic midterms sweep changes for U.S.-China relations

Democrats taking power from Republicans in Congress raises risks for U.S.-China tensions around Taiwan, while hardening tech competition, analysts said. Russia and China may feel the need to test the U.S. if they feel Trump is on "his way out," according to Robert Kagan, senior fellow at the Brookings Institution.

Moonshot turned to Europe for Nvidia Blackwell access amid U.S. China curbs, sources tell CNBC

Deals have been discussed this year for Moonshot to rent capacity from neoclouds hosting Nvidia graphics processing units, or GPUs, at facilities in Europe, four people familiar with the matter said. Meanwhile in Asia, reports of a prison sentencing in South Korea reflect the growing AI chip talent war.

#113. Children’s Place files for bankruptcy protection, seeks buyer

Children's clothing retailer The Children's Place has filed for chapter 11 bankruptcy protection, it said Sunday.

The company is looking to sell substantially all of its assets and is seeking offers from potential buyers by Dec. 9, with a goal to close a deal by the end of December.

The retailer, whose brands include The Children's Place and Gymboree, says it operates two digital storefronts and 514 North American stores, and has distribution in 13 countries through franchise and wholesale partners. The company's performance has taken a hit in recent years from tariff hikes and slower consumer spending, among other factors.

Its stores and e-commerce sites remain open for now, and the company is planning to continue its ongoing inventory sales with court approval.

"For nearly 60 years, The Children's Place has supported growing families with apparel that customers have come to know and love. We recognize the significance of this development for our employees, customers, vendors, and other partners, and we are committed to conducting an efficient and transparent sale process," said Rudy Morando, co-chief restructuring officer of The Children's Place. "In the meantime, we remain focused on serving the families who have trusted The Children's Place for generations."

This move comes days after interim Children's Place CEO Muhammad Asif Seemab and Chief Strategy Officer Muhammad Umair stepped down from their roles. Seemab and Umair will both remain on the company's board. CFO John Szczepanski took on the role of acting president and principal executive officer.

Seemab is also managing director of Saudi-based Mithaq Capital, which took a majority stake in The Children's Place in 2024 when the company was facing financial trouble.

Even with support from the investors, the company has not been able to significantly improve its financial outlook. In its bankruptcy filing, the company reported about $742 million in assets, compared to about $881 million in debts.

In the second fiscal quarter of 2026, its most recently reported quarter, the company's net sales dropped nearly 19% from the same period the year prior. At the time, Seemab said in a news release that during the quarter the company was "stabilizing our customer file" and "driving traffic to our stores and websites," and saw "lower conversion, combined with a very clearance-heavy mix."

He also pointed to tough comparisons to the prior-year period, due in part to back-to-school shopping demand that "was uniquely elevated ... ahead of anticipated tariff price increases."

The Children's Place stock has plummeted in recent years and was trading around 18 cents per share on Monday.

#110. Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Hundreds of billions of dollars have been wiped off the value of Nvidia after the US chipmaker announced an independent investigation would be carried out into its chief executive's affair with an alleged Chinese spy.

The tech firm saw its shares fall by about 4% by close of trade on Monday after Jensen Huang admitted having an extramarital affair.

Nvidia's probe came after reports that the US believed the Nvidia boss had been targeted in an espionage campaign by China.

However, China has rejected the claims, with its foreign ministry saying allegations about Chinese spies were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Nvidia said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised, but a statement by the firm's board of directors said it would begin an investigation in "light of recent reporting that founder and chief executive Jensen Huang has been the target of a foreign intelligence effort".

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report, external.

The report said Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington last spring warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia.

Jensen Huang admitted he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children. He added he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation.

The Nvidia co-founder has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week.

Members of the US House Foreign Affairs Committee last week asked Jensen Huang to appear for a closed-door briefing on his interactions with Alice Huang, as well as Nvidia's security protocols around espionage.

"Recent reports raise significant national security concerns regarding the PRC's [China's] access to sensitive US technology and information," congressmen Brian Mast, John Moolenaar, and Rick Crawford wrote in a letter.

"It is vital that Congress determines the extent to which US national security has been placed at risk."

After Nvidia's investigation was announced, other big tech bosses, including Elon Musk, came out in support of Jensen Huang to continue leading the firm.

US President Donald Trump said the Nvidia boss was an "honourable guy".

However, the news sparked a sell-off in Nvidia's shares, with about $250bn (Â£190bn) wiped of the company's market value.

While Nvidia shares fell, the US stock market opened the week on a positive note after last week's turmoil, with Nvidia's rival chipmakers benefiting.

But analysts suggested the hit to the company would be short-lived. Victoria Fernandez, chief market strategist at Crossmark Global Investments said investors would not abandon Nvidia, because the chipmaker was so important to the AI industry.

"Nvidia is the core of this AI trade, and without them, all these other pieces start to fall apart because the financing's not there," she told the BBC.

Jensen Huang co-founded Nvidia, which is central to making the hardware for emerging AI technologies, with two other engineers in 1993.

Its chips were initially designed for gaming, but he decided to turn its focus on AI development, turning it into the world's second most valuable company.

A key concern for investors going forward is what would happen to Nvidia if Jensen Huang was removed from the company, as Fernandez said there was "no natural successor" to the chief executive.

Jensen Huang, unlike other founders in the tech industry, is vulnerable to any move by Nvidia's board of directors to oust him.

He owns only around 3.5% of the company, and Nvidia operates on a single class voting structure, meaning his stake would not be enough to act as a veto power over any board decision.

By contrast, many tech founders who lead their companies, including Mark Zuckerberg of Meta and Musk of Tesla and SpaceX, hold significant percentages of multiple types of stock in their companies.

Fernandez said she does not believe the board will remove Jensen Huang as chief executive, unless there is glaring evidence of wrongdoing related to national security or intellectual property, "but I bet they start bringing someone up as his number two, to have there for the future."

The modest fall in its shares mean investors believe it is unlikely he will step down or be removed as chief executive, but that the outcome of the investigation will be closely watched.

Even people who do not directly own shares in Nvidia can be affected by rises and falls in its value. Pensions or savings invested in pots which have stakes in the company through index tracking funds can be impacted, for example.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Nvidia chips have been a major focus of the AI race between the US and China and the White House has previously imposed controls on where its advanced products can be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted by Washington over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Jensen Huang has been against restricting the sale of Nvidia's chips to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

Additional reporter by Samira Hussain, North America business correspondent.

#109. Nvidia's CEO turmoil gives newly empowered Democrats fresh ammunition in pursuit of oversight

Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang's close ties to the U.S. government were already poised to loosen with Democrats winning control of Congress and promising far more oversight of AI development than what the chip giant has experienced under Republican leadership.

That was before he admitted on Sunday to having an extramarital affair.

The CEO was romantically involved with Alice Huang, according to The Financial Times, who U.S. intelligence officials believe is a spy for China's Ministry of State Security, The New York Times reported. Alice Huang is also affiliated with an Nvidia customer called Megaspeed that's been accused of helping restricted Chinese companies acquire Nvidia's artificial intelligence chips.

Huang apologized to staffers in a note on Sunday for being "unfaithful to my wife," and said the company's board was investigating any related national security concerns. Nvidia's board said it has "confidence in Jensen," who co-founded the company in 1993 and has led it ever since, building a net worth of more than $150 billion in the process.

Even before last week's midterm elections, which saw Democrats win sweeping majorities in the House and Senate, vocal party leaders wanted answers from Huang about China's access to advanced Nvidia chips and his company's lobbying for favorable legislation. Sen. Elizabeth Warren of Massachusetts said days before the election, when the spy story was first surfacing, that "we must pass bipartisan legislation to keep our advanced AI technology out of China's hands." Months before that, in June, Huang declined an invitation from Warren to testify before the Senate Banking Committee.

Democratic House leader Hakeem Jeffries of New York, who is the presumed speaker of the House in the new Congress, told CBS News on Monday that it is "a significant issue of concern."

Jeffries said there is a classified briefing with White House officials later Monday, and he expects the Nvidia CEO's affair to be discussed. He also said he had not seen the memo from Secretary of State Marco Rubio about Jensen Huang's relationship with the alleged spy, which was reported by The New York Times.

With Democrats about to take the gavel, Delaware Sen. Chris Coons, who is in line to chair the Senate Foreign Relations Committee, wrote on X on Saturday that "Congress will investigate and get answers," following the allegations that appeared in a New York Times report Friday.

It's a sharp reversal for Huang, who gained first-name status with President Donald Trump and has been closely allied with the administration in trying to limit AI regulation. Nvidia shares plunged 12% in the two days after the election, pacing a drop in AI-related stocks, on fears that incoming lawmakers, at the federal and state levels, and a host of newly passed ballot measures will slow the buildout and halt the AI boom.

Huang is a singular voice in the AI industry, as his company's technology is used to train nearly all major AI models. He's also turned Nvidia into an essential source of financing for AI startups, and pulled together some of the biggest names on Wall Street into a group that announced plans in August to raise $500 billion for investing in the buildout.

At Nvidia, Huang is the sole public face of the company and has no clear second-in-command. He decides which projects are a strategic priority, and frequently sends short emails to individual engineers about details. Huang has said he has 60 direct reports and reviews every employee's salary. Meanwhile, he packs his calendar with meetings with world leaders and CEOs, alongside keynote speeches at conferences and trade shows.

"Nvidia is not an ordinary company and Jensen is not an ordinary CEO; he IS Nvidia," Bernstein analyst Stacy Rasgon wrote in a note on Sunday, adding that it's not clear who would succeed him.

Patrick Moorhead, CEO at Moor Insights & Strategy, said in an email that "Jensen holding so much power is why Nvidia moves faster than anyone."

Unlike many of his tech peers, Huang is not chairman of the board. Rather, he's one of 11 board members, and there's no designated chair.

One investor close to Huang told CNBC that, depending on what the board's investigation finds, he could foresee Huang taking a leave of absence.

"Today's news will not slow Nvidia," a company spokesperson said in a statement. "Jensen is committed to continuing to lead Nvidia as CEO without interruption."

But scrutiny of Nvidia is set to intensify on Wall Street and in Washington, where Huang's level of influence is almost certain to change.

He has been a major advocate for more chip exports to China, arguing that it's better for AI labs there to build on American technology than to encourage the development of domestic alternatives. Huang joined Trump on Air Force One in May on a China trip that's now raised additional questions about what the administration and Huang knew about Chinese spying at the time.

One question Democrats are likely to probe is whether the Trump administration was aware of Huang's relationship with the alleged Chinese spy, and whether the Nvidia CEO influenced American policy, said Sam Bresnick, a research fellow at the Center for Security and Emerging Technology.

"That is going to be very high up on the Democrats' lists of people to investigate to try to figure out to what extent was the Trump administration aware of what was going on," Bresnick said.

But pressure isn't just coming from Democrats. House Republican leaders have already asked Huang for a closed-door briefing on Chinese spying issues later this month.

Trump has continued to back Huang, telling reporters on Sunday that he's an "honorable guy" and has "done an amazing job for our country."

"I think he probably has an excuse for it," Trump said of the incident.

Amid all the noise about the election results and the spying revelations, Nvidia is facing a crucial stretch on the markets.

Neoclouds CoreWeave and Nebius, which are both backed by Nvidia and are big buyers of its chips, are scheduled to report earnings this week after their stocks lost roughly half their value over the past month. Investors are particularly concerned about their hefty debt loads and how they'll continue to fund their buildouts with interest rates rising and lenders turning skittish.

Nvidia has its own earnings report coming on Nov. 17, when Huang, assuming he sticks with the typical format, will face questions from analysts. And then there's the Anthropic initial public offering, which is critical for Nvidia as it's both an investor in the Claude creator and counts the AI lab as a significant customer.

A couple of weeks after earnings, Nvidia is hosting a large conference in Washington, D.C., where Huang is scheduled to give the keynote on Dec. 1.

Bullish investors and analysts can still point to the insatiable demand for Nvidia's technology. The company recently projected 70% sales growth in its upcoming fiscal year. Analysts polled by FactSet expect nearly $694 billion in fiscal 2028 sales.

"We believe demand for the company's products seems unlikely to be affected," Rasgon wrote. "But it seems plausible to postulate increased risks (regulatory etc.) as AI buildouts become an increasingly political issue."

— CNBC's Seema Mody, Katie Tarasov, Ashley Capoot and MacKenzie Sigalos contributed reporting to this report.

#108. Lucid misses Wall Street’s earnings expectations, expands loan from Saudi Arabia fund

Lucid Group missed Wall Street's third-quarter expectations as the embattled electric vehicle maker continues an "operational reset" aimed at cutting costs and narrowing losses.

The company on Monday also announced a $1.75 billion increase in a delayed draw term loan from Saudi Arabia's Public Investment Fund, the company's largest shareholder.

Lucid stock fell roughly 5% in aftermarket trading Monday.

Here's how Lucid performed in the third quarter compared with average estimates compiled by LSEG:

Despite the top- and bottom-line misses, the company's adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of a loss of $726 million beat Wall Street's expectations of a loss of $611 million for the third quarter. Its net loss for the quarter was $830.9 million, down from a loss of $978.4 million a year earlier.

Lucid's 2025 third quarter included revenue of $336.6 million, a loss of $3.31 per share and an adjusted EBITDA loss of $717.7 million.

The quarterly results are the first since Lucid CEO Silvio Napoli, who started leading the company in June, announced a restructuring plan that included implementing several cost-cutting efforts and delaying the company's upcoming midsize vehicle from the end of this year until the second half of next.

"Our transformation is beginning to show progress. We are acting with urgency, strengthening the fundamentals and driving greater discipline, while continuing to invest in the priorities to deliver profitable long-term growth," Napoli said in a release.

The company's turnaround plan includes $1.4 billion in cash flow improvements this year as well as focusing attention on a robotaxi program with Uber and Nuro, a factory that's under construction in Saudi Arabia and the midsize vehicle.

Lucid said the capital from PIF is expected to give the company "additional financial flexibility as Lucid delivers on its turnaround program." It is expected to assist in funding "operations and key investments" into the fourth quarter of 2027, including the start of production for the midsize vehicle.

The additional funds boost Lucid's total liquidity to $3.75 billion, up from $2 billion to end the third quarter.

"We are grateful for the continued financial support from our anchor shareholder, which further energizes us to remain focused on execution and on our commitments as we build a stronger, sustainable Lucid," Napoli said.

Lucid said Monday that the company expects to communicate its midterm plan and targets early next year. The EV maker in May suspended its guidance and many targets, citing new leadership and time needed to develop a turnaround plan.

#105. Trump says FBI investigating Nvidia boss's affair with alleged Chinese spy

"He's suffering": Trump answers BBC question about Nvidia's Jensen Huang

Donald Trump has said the FBI is investigating the affair between the boss of Nvidia and an alleged Chinese spy.

The US President said he spoke with chief executive Jensen Huang on Monday following reports that the US believed he had been targeted in a Chinese espionage campaign.

"I asked him that question, 'Did you have any idea she was a spy?' He said 'I had no idea she was a spy' and I really believe him," Trump told reporters.

Beijing has rejected claims that Huang was targeted, with its foreign ministry saying the allegations were "entirely groundless, speculative and intended to smear and malign China".

Nvidia, who has commissioned an independent investigation into the affair involving Alice Huang, alleged to be a Chinese spy, said it had no reason to believe the company's secrets or national security were compromised.

The revelations knocked about $250bn (Â£190bn) off the value of Nvidia during trading on Monday as the firm's share price fell by about 4%.

Trump later told reporters at the White House that the FBI was now looking into the issue, but did not elaborate on the probe or when it was launched. The FBI declined to comment when contacted the BBC.

US intelligence officials believe Alice Huang, also known as Huang Le, was a spy for Beijing, according to a New York Times report.

The report said Secretary of State Marco Rubio sent a memo to top officials in Washington last spring warning that the Nvidia boss was in a close relationship with a Chinese spy.

Alice Huang was managing director of Singaporean company Megaspeed, which has had dealings with Nvidia, the company at the centre at the development of AI worldwide through its microchips.

Trump's remarks are the first indication of whether Huang knew about the woman's alleged ties to Chinese espionage.

The American firm run, co-founded by Huang, is also a major focus in the AI rivalry between the US and China, with Washington officials treating the company's exports to Beijing as a matter of national security.

At the White House, Trump was asked multiple times, including by the BBC, on when he was made aware of the relationship. He said he was informed "over a period of time" but was not specific.

Trump said he thought Huang was an "honorable guy and I feel very confident that he will be in good shape" after the investigations.

"I hope there's no problem, I believe he is suffering greatly," the president said.

He said he spoke with Huang on the phone on Monday "just to see if he's feeling alright, how you doing, and I asked him about that and he said he absolutely had no idea that the person in question was a spy".

Nvidia designs advanced computer chips that power AI models and lies at the centre of the US and China's technology race - a key reason why Huang's affair has sparked national security fears.

Trump has raised concerns that allowing China to lead in AI would supercharge Beijing's military and surveillance capabilities.

The president added he was "sure" Chinese leader Xi Jinping would "ask me about it" when they next met.

Huang has admitted he was "unfaithful" to his wife and apologised for hurting her and their children.

He said on Monday that he was "committed to protecting Nvidia and supporting America's national security" and would cooperate with the investigation the company had launched.

Huang has also been requested to appear for a joint closed-door briefing for several Congressional committees next week.

In the letter, the chairmen of the committees said they were seeking a briefing "on Nvidia's security protocols against foreign adversary espionage and your interactions with a publicly reported People's Republic of China (PRC) intelligence asset: Megaspeed executive Alice Huang".

It is unclear whether Huang will appear. Nvidia has declined to comment, while the the BBC has also contacted the committees.

What we know so far about Jensen Huang's affair with suspected Chinese spy

Zoe Kleinman: Nvidia CEO's affair will cause ripples across US and beyond

Nvidia shares tumble over investigation into Huang's affair with alleged Chinese spy

Nvidia has been around for decades, with Huang founding it with two other engineers in 1993, but its rise to one of the world's most valuable and powerful companies has only been recent.

It originally designed computer chips for gaming, but its decision to shift focus to AI development turned it into the powerhouse it is today.

Its chips have become so valuable to the AI-build out that the White House previously imposed controls on where its advanced products could be sold.

The H200, Nvidia's second most advanced semiconductor, had previously been restricted over concerns that it would give China's technology industry an edge over the US.

But Huang has long been against restricting sales to China, arguing that it would be better for the global rollout of AI to be powered by US technology.

In January, the Trump administration said it would allow the company to sell its H200 chips to Chinese customers under certain conditions.

#104. Trump questions midterm election results, revives voting-machine conspiracy

President Donald Trump on Monday questioned the legitimacy of the midterm election results, resurrecting his pattern of raising doubts about electoral losses for himself and other Republicans.

Trump's social media post raising questions about the election came six days after his fellow Republicans suffered historic defeats at the polls nationwide, including in several GOP strongholds like Texas and South Carolina.

"Does anybody really believe that this 'Election' was honest," Trump wrote on Truth Social.

"How could the Polls be so wrong??? Some early voting 'dumps' were so large that it was impossible to win, no matter how many people went to vote," the president said. "It's all about the machines, which are far more important than the voter!"

Trump's post was a sharp pivot from what has been a conciliatory tone after last Tuesday's thumping of Republican candidates, which resulted in Democrats regaining majorities in both the Senate and House of Representatives.

On Election Night, Trump told The New York Times that he was "absolutely willing" to accept the results of the midterms, and that he was willing to work with Democrats.

"We'll, you know, look forward to working with them," Trump told The Times. "We'll probably work something out."

The next day, Trump said, "I'll get along probably with the Democrats unless they want to be destructive."

Later in the week, he announced he was replacing White House Chief of Staff Susie Wiles with her deputy, Stephen Miller, the architect of the administration's aggressive immigration enforcement crackdown, which has drawn widespread criticism from Democrats.

Sen. Chuck Schumer, a New York Democrat who could be the majority leader in the Senate in January, said in a post on X, "Donald Trump's pathetic return to election denialism was inevitable."

"He can whine all he wants, but nothing will stop this inescapable truth: For the last two years of his presidency, Trump will face the largest Democratic congressional majorities in decades, all because he has been an unprecedented failure," Schumer said.

After his electoral loss to former President Joe Biden in 2020, Trump argued, without evidence, that he only lost as a result of widespread ballot fraud.

Trump's claims, which were joined by dozens of unsuccessful court cases challenging election procedures, culminated in the Jan. 6, 2021, attack on the U.S. Capitol by a mob of his supporters after he spoke at a rally outside the White House.

The riot disrupted a joint session of Congress for hours. Later that day, Congress confirmed Biden's election by the Electoral College.

Critics of Trump's conspiracy theories about elections have noted that he lost the 2020 race when he was president, but won the 2024 presidential election, when Biden was in the White House.

They also noted that in the past week, Republicans had control of states that saw major Democratic wins that few analysts would have predicted a year ago, including Texas, Kansas, South Carolina, and Iowa.

#101. PepsiCo to cut about 1,000 jobs as North American business struggles

PepsiCo is cutting about 1,000 jobs across North America as the company tries to turn around lagging sales in its home market.

"We have a clear ambition for PepsiCo North America: to build a stronger, more future-fit business that delivers industry-leading growth," Ram Krishnan, CEO of PepsiCo North America, wrote in a Monday letter to employees obtained by CNBC. "To do that, we need to be faster, more connected, and more capable, while continuing to invest in growth — behind our people, capabilities and brands."

Over the next week, Pepsi plans to cut jobs as it says it aims to streamline its operations and better connect teams across the company's North American business. PepsiCo employs about 306,000 people worldwide, including roughly 125,000 in the United States, as of Dec. 27, according to a company filing.

The Wall Street Journal first reported the coming layoffs.

In October, PepsiCo cut its full-year earnings outlook, citing the struggles in its home market. During the third quarter, its North American beverage unit saw volume shrink 2%, while its North American food division reported flat volume.

While the company has tried to boost sales by cutting prices and investing in marketing, the turnaround is taking longer than expected, Chief Financial Officer Steve Schmitt said on the company's earnings conference call.

Shares of Pepsi rose slightly in afternoon trading. The stock is down nearly 1% this year, lagging behind the S&P 500's gains of nearly 11% over the same period.

#97. Skydance outlines its $6 billion in planned cost savings. Here's the breakdown

Skydance is pulling back the curtain on how it plans to achieve at least $6 billion in cost savings in the next three years.

"By combining two global streaming services, two film and TV studio groups, two television portfolios, and overlapping corporate functions, we can reduce duplicative spend across an expense base of more than $50 billion," the company wrote in a letter to shareholders Monday as part of Paramount Skydance's third-quarter earnings release.

Skydance, the company formed by the combination of Paramount Skydance and Warner Bros. Discovery, said it projects at least $2.2 billion in cost reductions from eliminating "redundant back-office and corporate functions," including eliminating management layers and ramping the use of artificial intelligence. Those savings also account for "rationalizing" its office footprint.

Skydance expects at least $1 billion in savings from streamlining marketing and promotional spending. And around $1.4 billion in savings will come from unifying its streaming businesses and broadcast architecture and integrating back-end technology.

A final $1.4 billion in savings will be tied to "optimizing third-party spend," eliminating overlapping expenses, and consolidating purchasing decisions to one centralized group, Skydance said.

"We are confident in achieving these savings, drawing on our playbook from Paramount," the company wrote. "That program is on track to achieve over $2.7 billion in run-rate efficiencies by the end of 2026."

Some of that cost-cutting has already begun.

Last month, Skydance began offering voluntary buyouts to employees ahead of official layoffs. At the same time, the company adopted a five-day-per-week in-office policy. Additionally, last week saw the departure of eight executives — four from Warner Bros. and four from Paramount.

The company has previously said it plans to eventually merge the Paramount+ and HBO Max streaming services into a single platform.

On Monday, Skydance said it would take the first step toward that goal early next year, offering a bundle of the streaming services "at an even greater value."

Co-CEO Ynon Kreiz told investors during the company's earnings call that Skydance is also looking for ways to optimize its content budget. The company spends more than $30 billion annually on shows and films.

"As one company, we can make smarter choices about how we distribute and monetize both new content and library content," Kreiz said.

Skydance noted that it's expecting a $200 million headwind in the fourth quarter from theatrical underperformances at Warner Bros. studio related to already launched titles and upcoming releases.

The company will also utilize AI to speed up production and decrease costs to make films, co-CEO and chairman David Ellison said on the call.

"We think these tools will help unlock more original storytelling by being able to make the budget more digestible to be able to take that amount of risk," Ellison said.

The updates came alongside third-quarter results for the legacy Paramount Skydance company that included revenue of $7.03 billion, which was roughly in line with StreetAccount estimates of $7.04 billion, and adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $1 billion, topping StreetAccount estimates of $913.5 million.

However, adjusted earnings per share of 45 cents were not immediately comparable to StreetAccount EPS estimates.

The results don't include the impact of the Warner Bros. Discovery acquisition, which closed after the end of the third quarter.

Skydance detailed business units of the combined entity, including studios, direct-to-consumer and TV, and said it will provide full-year guidance during its fourth-quarter earnings report early next year.

"Going forward, we will provide guidance on an annual basis, as we prioritize long-term value creation, which we believe will be more consistent with our strategic approach," the company told shareholders. "We will continue to provide quarterly updates, including certain forward-looking commentary where applicable, which will give investors visibility into our near-term progress of the business."

#93. 3 unexpected reasons the October jobs report was so strong

A little-noticed category in Friday's blockbuster jobs report is challenging the consensus view about job losses caused by artificial intelligence.

At least for now, the data suggests the U.S. is gaining jobs as a result of the AI boom, not losing them.

And another category suggests the strong jobs numbers could have some staying power.

Nonfarm payrolls grew by 165,000 jobs in October, according to the Friday report from the Bureau of Labor Statistics. It was a surprising surge in hiring that blew way past Wall Street's expectations. Economists polled by Dow Jones had forecast growth of just 59,000 for the month.

Here are three surprising things you might have missed from the report.

Hiring for consultants surged in the month, sending the profession's total head count to a fresh record.

The labor force for management, scientific and technical consultants expanded by 17,300 jobs between September and October, the largest month-over-month increase in nearly two years. This category is a subset of the professional and business services sector tracked by BLS and accounted for 25% of the entire sector's gains for the month.

What's more, this workforce has grown by more than 45,000 jobs over the past year. That gain pushed the total employee base for these types of workers past 1.9 million for the first time on record, according to BLS data.

Hiring in the industry had largely stalled since 2023 following years of rapid expansion during the pandemic. Wall Street has questioned if the rise of artificial intelligence will curb demand for these services.

Those fears have led shares of consulting firm Gartner to lose more than half of their value over the past three years. Accenture and FTI Consulting have dropped about 18% and 9% over that period, even as the S&P 500 has climbed roughly 74%.

Now, some economists are speculating that the recent jump could be driven by businesses hiring consultants to help them adopt AI. Gartner forecast in September that worldwide AI spending would total $2.7 trillion in 2026, a nearly 50% increase year over year.

"The AI buildout and corporate interests to increase utilization will keep the technical consultants employed," said Jeffrey Roach, chief economist at LPL Financial.

Roach called the consulting sector a bright spot in October that is "worth watching." He noted that these jobs also saw double-digit growth in the late 1990s, as firms raced to implement new internet technology into their businesses.

So far, data suggests that AI may be adding more jobs to the labor market than it is taking away through automation. An assessment by Moody's Analytics found that AI has created a net 786,000 jobs since ChatGPT launched in November 2022, driven in part by data center construction.

"Wasn't AI supposed to destroy jobs? The most dystopian scenarios even called for mass unemployment," Mark Zandi, Moody's chief economist, wrote in a social media post on Monday. "That risk hasn't gone away, and AI-driven productivity gains could still shift into a higher gear. But for now, AI is adding to job growth, not subtracting from it."

Hiring of temporary workers also drove growth, which could signal that the labor market is heating up.

The subsector's payrolls grew by 18,600 in October, one of its largest month-over-month increases since 2022. This category is another part of the professional and business services sector and accounted for 27% of the sector's gains for the month.

The total size of this workforce is still well below record highs set in 2022. But headcount has grown roughly 3% since December and now sits at levels last seen in April 2025, the month that President Donald Trump's rollout of his plan for steep tariffs put corporate America on alert.

Economists see temporary hiring as a leading indicator in the labor market. Conventional wisdom suggests that companies that hire new workers on a short-term basis often transition them to full-time roles later.

That could be what's happening here, but the fact that the end of the year is approaching makes it harder to tell. The boost could be a result of hiring as the holiday shopping season approaches — and if employers are staffing up for the holidays, they are less likely to retain those workers when January rolls around. Such hiring hasn't typically happened this early, but the season creeps earlier each year. The BLS adjusts its numbers to account for seasonal trends, but won't immediately make an adjustment if the trend is changing.

One hint that holiday hiring may be shifting comes in a separate category of workers: couriers and messengers. This is another area that acts as a barometer for holiday labor demand, and just over one out of every 10 new jobs in the month was in this category.

That category "bears watching for reversal in the months ahead," said Jason Pride, chief of investment strategy and research at Glenmede, a wealth manager.

Hiring related to political campaigns in the final weeks of an expensive election cycle could have also boosted payrolls.

Employment in political organizations has historically spiked heading into midterm or presidential elections, according to a BLS analysis. Staffing levels tend to peak in October of those years, the data shows. Nonfarm payrolls expanded in the months before the 2018 and 2022 midterm elections.

But the vast size of campaign coffers this cycle may have translated into more jobs than before.

The 2026 congressional election is slated to go down in history as the most expensive midterm on record, according to an analysis from OpenSecrets, a money-in-politics watchdog group. Billions of dollars spent on campaigns may have translated into paid gigs that involve door knocking, phone banking or other election-related work.
